MATBG as Topological Heavy Fermion: I. Exact Mapping and Correlated Insulators
Zhi-Da Song, B. Andrei Bernevig

Abstract
Magic-angle ( θ = 1.05 ∘ \theta=1.05^{\circ} ) twisted bilayer graphene (MATBG) has shown two seemingly contradictory characters: the localization and quantum-dot-like behavior in STM experiments, and delocalization in transport experiments. We construct a model, which naturally captures the two aspects, from the Bistritzer-MacDonald (BM) model in a first principle spirit . A set of local flat-band orbitals ( f f ) centered at the AA-stacking regions are responsible to the localization. A set of extended topological semi-metallic conduction bands ( c c ), which are at small energetic separation from the local orbitals, are responsible to the delocalization and transport. The topological flat bands of the BM model appear as a result of the hybridization of f f - and c c -electrons. This model then provides a new perspective for the strong correlation physics, which is now described as strongly correlated f f -electrons coupled to nearly free c c -electrons - we hence name our model as the topological heavy fermion model . Using this model, we obtain the U(4) and U(4) × \times U(4) symmetries of Refs. Kang and Vafek 2019 ; Bultinck et al. 2020a ; Vafek and Kang 2020 ; Bernevig et al. 2021a ; Lian et al. 2021 as well as the correlated insulator phases and their energies. Simple rules for the ground states and their Chern numbers are derived. Moreover, features such as the large dispersion of the charge ± 1 \pm 1 excitations Bernevig et al. 2021b ; Vafek and Kang 2021 ; Bultinck et al. 2020a , and the minima of the charge gap at the Γ M \Gamma_{M} point can now, for the first time, be understood both qualitatively and quantitatively in a simple physical picture. Our mapping opens the prospect of using heavy-fermion physics machinery to the superconducting physics of MATBG.

efforts have been made to understand the nature of these exotic phases.
Theoretical challenges for understanding the correlation physics come from both the strong interaction compared to relatively small band width as well as from the topology
Liu et al. 2019a
;
Song et al. 2019
;
Po et al. 2019
;
Song et al. 2021
;
Tarnopolsky et al. 2019
;
Hejazi et al. 2019a
, which forbids a symmetric lattice description of the problem.
The two flat bands of MATBG posses strong topology in the presence of
C_{2z}T
(time-reversal followed by
C_{2z}
rotation) and particle-hole (
P
) symmetries
Song et al. 2021
, which supersedes the earlier,
C_{2z}T
symmetry-protected fragile topology
Song et al. 2019
;
Po et al. 2019
. This strong topology extends to the entire continuum BM model, and implies the absence of a lattice model for any number of bands.
The topology is also responsible to exotic phases such as quantum anomalous Hall states
Bultinck et al. 2020b
;
Liu and Dai 2021
;
Lian et al. 2021
;
Wu and Das Sarma 2020
;
Bultinck et al. 2020a
;
Kwan et al. 2021
and fractional Chern states
Sheffer and Stern 2021
;
Repellin and Senthil 2020
;
Abouelkomsan et al. 2020
;
Ledwith et al. 2020
.

Two types of complementary strategies have been proposed to resolve the problem of the lattice description. One is to construct extended Hubbard models
Po et al. 2018a
;
Kang and Vafek 2018
;
Kang and Vafek 2019
;
Koshino et al. 2018
;
Po et al. 2019
;
Vafek and Kang 2021
;
Zou et al. 2018
;
Xu et al. 2018
;
Yuan and Fu 2018
, where either
C_{2z}T

Kang and Vafek 2018
;
Kang and Vafek 2019
;
Koshino et al. 2018
;
Vafek and Kang 2021
;
Yuan and Fu 2018
or
P

Po et al. 2019
becomes non-local in real space.
The other is to adopt a full momentum-space formalism
Lian et al. 2021
;
Bernevig et al. 2021b
;
Xie et al. 2021
;
Zhang et al. 2021
;
Bultinck et al. 2020a
;
Cea and Guinea 2020
;
Zhang et al. 2020
;
Hofmann et al. 2021
, where locality becomes hidden.
(Besides the two strategies, some phenomenological models are also proposed
Efimkin and MacDonald 2018
;
Wu et al. 2019
;
Xu and Balents 2018
;
Thomson et al. 2018
;
Classen et al. 2019
;
Eugenio and Dağ 2020
;
Repellin et al. 2020
;
Fernandes and Venderbos 2020
.)
The real and momentum space strong coupling models elucidated the nature of the correlated insulator states: they are ferromagnets - sometimes carrying Chern numbers - in a large U(4) or U(4)
\times
U(4) symmetry space that contains spin, valley and band quantum number
Kang and Vafek 2019
;
Bultinck et al. 2020a
;
Bernevig et al. 2021a
.
The dispersion of the excitations above the correlated insulators
Bernevig et al. 2021b
;
Vafek and Kang 2021
;
Bultinck et al. 2020a
- where superconductivity appears upon doping - is, despite being exact - not physically understood.

In the current manuscript, nevertheless, we find it possible to write down a fully symmetric model that has a simple real space picture, which, remarkably and elegantly, solves the aforementioned puzzles. We reformulate and map the interacting MATBG as an effective topological heavy fermion system, which consists of local orbitals (
f
) centered at the AA-stacking regions and delocalized topological conduction bands (
c
).
The
f
-electrons are so localized that they have an almost zero kinetic energy (
\sim 0.1
meV) but a strong on-site Coulomb repulsion that we compute to be
\sim 60
meV.
The
c
-electrons carry the symmetry anomaly and have unbounded kinetic energies.
The actual flat bands of the BM model are from a hybridization (
\sim
20meV) between the
f
- and
c
-bands.
The interacting Hamiltonian also couples the
f
and
c
electrons through the presence of several types of interactions.
Using this model, the ground states
Po et al. 2018a
;
Padhi et al. 2018
;
Bultinck et al. 2020a
;
Kang and Vafek 2019
;
Liu and Dai 2021
;
Ochi et al. 2018
;
Venderbos and Fernandes 2018
;
Dodaro et al. 2018
;
Seo et al. 2019
;
Pixley and Andrei 2019
;
Xie and MacDonald 2020b
;
Cea and Guinea 2020
;
Zhang et al. 2020
and their topologies can be understood in a simple, physical picture.
The quasi-particle excitation bandwidth can even be analytically determined.

), respectively.

\theta=1.05^{\circ}
, where the moiré BZ and high symmetry momenta are illustrated in the upper inset panel. The overlaps between the Bloch states and the trial WFs are represented by the red circles. The density profile of the constructed maximally localized WFs (
f

(c) Bands given by the topological heavy fermion model (black lines) compared to the BM bands (blue crosses).
The
c
- (blue) and
f
-bands (red) in the decoupled limit, where
\gamma=v_{\star}^{\prime}=0
, are shown in the inset. Orange dashed lines indicate evolution of energy levels as
f
-
c
coupling is turned on.

Topological heavy fermion model —

The single-valley BM model has the symmetry of the magnetic space group
P6^{\prime}2^{\prime}2
, generated by
C_{3z}
,
C_{2x}
,
C_{2z}T
, and moiré translations.
(See Refs.
Song et al. 2019
;
SM
for this group and its irreducible representations - irreps.)
The energy bands in the valley
\eta=+
of the BM model are shown in
fig.

1
(b), where the bands are labeled by their irreps.
Refs.
Song et al. 2019
;
Po et al. 2019
showed that the irreps formed by the two flat bands,
i.e.
,
\Gamma_{1}\oplus\Gamma_{2}
;
M_{1}\oplus M_{2}
;
K_{2}K_{3}
, are not consistent with any local orbitals (band representations
Bradlyn et al. 2017
) and indicate a fragile
Po et al. 2018b
;
Ahn et al. 2019
;
Bouhon et al. 2019
;
Cano et al. 2018
topological obstruction to a two-band lattice model.
Here we resolve the fragile topology by involving higher energy bands.
Suppose we can “borrow” a
\Gamma_{3}
irrep from higher (
\sim
20meV) energy bands and use it to replace the
\Gamma_{1}\oplus\Gamma_{2}
states; then the replaced irreps -
\Gamma_{3}
,
M_{1}\oplus M_{2}
,
K_{2}K_{3}
- are consistent with
p_{x}\pm ip_{y}
orbitals located at the triangular lattice.
We hence introduce two trial Gaussian-type Wannier functions (WFs) that transform as
p_{x}\pm ip_{y}
orbitals under the crystalline symmetries.
As indicated by the overlaps between the trial WFs and the Bloch bands (
fig.

1
(a)), the trial WFs are supported by the flat band states at
\mathbf{k}
away from
\Gamma_{M}
and by the lowest higher energy band states around
\Gamma_{M}
.
Feeding the overlaps into the program Wannier90
Marzari and Vanderbilt 1997
;
Souza et al. 2001
;
Pizzi et al. 2020
, we obtain the corresponding maximally localized WFs, density profile of which is shown in
fig.

1
(b)
SM
.
(Similar local states are also discussed using different methods in Refs.
Liu et al. 2019a
;
Calderón and Bascones 2020
.)
These WFs are extremely localized - their nearest neighbor hoppings are about 0.1meV - and span 96% percent of the flat bands.

To recover the irreps and topology of the middle two bands, we have to take into account the remaining 4% states, without which the localized electrons could not form a superconductor.
To do this, we define the projector into the WFs as
\mathbb{P}
, the projector into the lowest six bands (per spin valley) as
\mathbb{I}
, and divide the low energy BM Hamiltonian
H_{BM}
into four parts:
H^{(f)}=\mathbb{P}H_{BM}\mathbb{P}
,
H^{(c)}=\mathbb{Q}H_{BM}\mathbb{Q}
,
H^{(fc)}=\mathbb{P}H_{BM}\mathbb{Q}
,
H^{(cf)}=H^{(fc)\dagger}
, where
\mathbb{Q}=\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{P}
,
H^{(c)}
is the remaining Hamiltonian, and
H^{(fc)}+h.c.
is the coupling between WFs and the remaining states.
As the couplings between WFs are extremely weak (
\sim
0.1meV) we find
H^{(f)}\approx 0
.
Since the two states in
\mathbb{P}
form
\Gamma_{3}
at
\Gamma_{M}
, the four states in
\mathbb{Q}
must form
\Gamma_{3}\oplus\Gamma_{1}\oplus\Gamma_{2}
at
\Gamma_{M}
due to the irrep counting.
Due to the crystalline and
P
symmetries,
H^{(c)}
in the valley
\eta
takes the form
SM

H^{(c,\eta)}(\mathbf{k})=\begin{pmatrix}0_{2\times 2}&v_{\star}(\eta k_{x}\sigma_{0}+ik_{y}\sigma_{z})\\
v_{\star}(\eta k_{x}\sigma_{0}-ik_{y}\sigma_{z})&M\sigma_{x}\end{pmatrix}

(1)

to linear order of
\mathbf{k}
, where the first two-by-two block is spanned by the
\Gamma_{3}
states and the second two-by-two block is spanned by the
\Gamma_{1}\oplus\Gamma_{2}
states.
The
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
states are split by the
M
term (blue bands in
fig.

1
(c)), while the
\Gamma_{3}
states form a quadratic touching at
\mathbf{k}=0
, which is shown in Ref.
SM
responsible to the symmetry anomaly
Song et al. 2021
jointly protected by
C_{2z}T
and
P
.
The coupling
H^{(fc)}
in the valley
\eta
has the form

H^{(fc,\eta)}(\mathbf{k})=\begin{pmatrix}\gamma\sigma_{0}+v_{\star}^{\prime}(\eta k_{x}\sigma_{x}+k_{y}\sigma_{y}),&0_{2\times 2}\end{pmatrix}\ ,

(2)

where the second block is computed to be extremely small and hence is omitted and written as
0_{2\times 2}
.

H^{(fc,\eta)}
will gap
H^{(c,\eta)}
, and hence provides for both the single particle gap and for the flat band topology of the BM model.
Using a set of usually adopt parameters for MATBG, we find
v_{\star}=-4.303\mathrm{eV}\cdot\mathrm{\mathring{A}}
,
M=3.697
meV,
\gamma=-24.75
meV,
v_{\star}^{\prime}=1.622\mathrm{eV}\cdot\mathrm{\mathring{A}}
.

Since the WFs and the remaining “
c
” degrees of freedom have localized and plane-wave-like wave functions, respectively, we make the analogy with local orbitals and conduction bands in heavy fermion systems.
We refer to them as local
f
-orbitals and (topological) conduction
c
-bands, respectively.
We use
f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}
(
\alpha=1,2
,
\eta=\pm
,
s=\uparrow,\downarrow
) to represent the annihilation operator of the
\alpha
-th WF of the valley
\eta
and spin
s
at the moiré unit cell
\mathbf{R}
. We use
c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s}
(
a=1,2,3,4
) to represent the annihilation operator of the
a
-th conduction band basis of the valley
\eta
and spin
s
at the moiré momentum
\mathbf{k}
.
The single-particle Hamiltonian can be written as

\displaystyle{\hat{H}}_{0}=

\displaystyle\sum_{|\mathbf{k}|<\Lambda_{c}}\sum_{aa^{\prime}\eta s}H^{(c,\eta)}_{aa^{\prime}}(\mathbf{k})c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k}a^{\prime}\eta s}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{k}|<\Lambda_{c}\\
\mathbf{R}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\alpha a\eta s}\Big(

\displaystyle e^{i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{R}-\frac{|\mathbf{k}|^{2}\lambda^{2}}{2}}H^{(fc,\eta)}_{\alpha a}(\mathbf{k})f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s}+h.c.\Big)\ ,

(3)

where
\Lambda_{c}
is the momentum cutoff for the
c
-electrons,
a_{M}
is the moiré lattice constant,
N
is the number of moiré unit cell in the system, and
\lambda
, which is found to be
0.3375a_{M}
, is a damping factor proportional to the size of WFs.
We plot the band structure of
{\hat{H}}_{0}
in
fig.

1
(c), where the splitting of the two
\Gamma_{3}
states is given by
2|\gamma|
and the bandwidth of the two flat bands is given by
2M\approx 7.4\text{meV}
.
The spectrum of
{\hat{H}}_{0}
matches very well with the BM model (
fig.

1
(a)) in the energy range [-70meV, 70meV].

The U(4) symmetry —

The projected model of MATBG
Kang and Vafek 2019
;
Bultinck et al. 2020a
;
Bernevig et al. 2021a
is found to possess a U(4) symmetry if the kinetic energy of the flat bands is omitted.
In the heavy fermion basis, this U(4) symmetry can be realized by imposing the flat band condition,
i.e.
,
M=0
.
(Note that
2|M|
is the bandwidth of the flat bands.)
The U(4) moments of the
f
-electrons,
\Gamma_{3}

c
-electrons, and
\Gamma_{1}\oplus\Gamma_{2}

c
-electrons are given by
SM

\displaystyle{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(f,\xi)}(\mathbf{R})=

\displaystyle\frac{\delta_{\xi,(-1)^{\alpha-1}\eta}}{2}A^{\mu\nu}_{\alpha\eta s,\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}

(4)

\displaystyle{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(c\prime,\xi)}(\mathbf{q})=

\displaystyle\frac{\delta_{\xi,(-1)^{a-1}\eta}}{2N}A^{\mu\nu}_{a\eta s,a^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}c_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{q}a\eta s}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k}a^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}},\;(a=1,2)

\displaystyle{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(c\prime\prime,\xi)}(\mathbf{q})=

\displaystyle\frac{\delta_{\xi,(-1)^{a-1}\eta}}{2N}B^{\mu\nu}_{a\eta s,a^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}c_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{q}a\eta s}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k}a^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}},\;(a=3,4)

respectively, where repeated indices should be summed over and
A^{\mu\nu},B^{\mu\nu}
(
\mu,\nu=0,x,y,z
) are eight-by-eight matrices

\displaystyle A^{\mu\nu}=

\displaystyle\{\sigma_{0}\tau_{0}{\varsigma}_{\nu},\sigma_{y}\tau_{x}{\varsigma}_{\nu},\sigma_{y}\tau_{y}{\varsigma}_{\nu},\sigma_{0}\tau_{z}{\varsigma}_{\nu}\}

(5)

\displaystyle B^{\mu\nu}=

\displaystyle\{\sigma_{0}\tau_{0}{\varsigma}_{\nu},-\sigma_{y}\tau_{x}{\varsigma}_{\nu},-\sigma_{y}\tau_{y}{\varsigma}_{\nu},\sigma_{0}\tau_{z}{\varsigma}_{\nu}\}\ ,

with
\sigma_{0,x,y,z}
,
\tau_{0,x,y,z}
,
{\varsigma}_{0,x,y,z}
being the Pauli or identity matrices for the orbital, valley, and spin degrees of freedom, respectively.
The
\pm 1
valued index
\xi
, equal to
(-1)^{\alpha-1}\eta
or
(-1)^{a-1}\eta
in the moments, labels different fundamental representations of the U(4) group.
The global U(4) rotations are generated by
{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}=\sum_{\xi=\pm 1}{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(f,\xi)}+{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(c\prime,\xi)}+{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(c\prime\prime,\xi)}
.
Unlike the U(4) rotations found in Refs.
Kang and Vafek 2019
;
Bultinck et al. 2020a
;
Bernevig et al. 2021a
, which only commute the projected Hamiltonian into the flat bands, the U(4) rotations here commute with the full Hamiltonian.
(Generators of the U(4) or U(4)
\times
U(4) symmetry in the first chiral limit
Tarnopolsky et al. 2019
;
Wang et al. 2021
is also given in Ref.
SM
.)

Interaction Hamiltonian —
To obtain the interaction Hamiltonian in the heavy fermion basis, we can first express the density operator
\rho(\mathbf{r})
of the BM model in terms of
f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}
and
c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s}
, and then substitute it into the Coulomb interaction,
\rho(\mathbf{r})V(\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{r}^{\prime})\rho(\mathbf{r}^{\prime})
.
By evaluating the Coulomb integrals, we obtain the interaction Hamiltonian resembling a periodic Anderson model with extra
f
-
c
exchange interactions
SM
,

{\hat{H}}_{I}={\hat{H}}_{U_{1}}+{\hat{H}}_{J}+{\hat{H}}_{U_{2}}+{\hat{H}}_{V}+{\hat{H}}_{W}\ .

(6)

{\hat{H}}_{U_{1}}=\frac{U_{1}}{2}\sum_{\mathbf{R}}:\rho^{f}_{\mathbf{R}}::\rho^{f}_{\mathbf{R}}:
are the on-site interactions of
f
-electrons, where
\rho^{f}_{\mathbf{R}}=\sum_{\alpha\eta s}f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}
is the
f
-electrons density and the colon symbols represent the normal ordered operator with respect to the normal state.

{\hat{H}}_{J}=-J\sum_{\mathbf{R}\mathbf{q}}\sum_{\mu\nu}\sum_{\xi=\pm}e^{-i\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{R}}:{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(f,\xi)}(\mathbf{R})::{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(c\prime\prime,\xi)}(\mathbf{q}):

(7)

is a ferromagnetic exchange coupling between U(4) moments of
f
-electrons and
\Gamma_{1}\oplus\Gamma_{2}

c
-electrons.
Using practical parameters for MATBG, we obtain
U_{1}=57.95
meV and
J=16.38
meV.
The other three terms in
{\hat{H}}_{I}
are:

H_{U_{2}}
- repulsion (
\sim 2.3
meV) between nearest neighbor
f
-electrons,

H_{V}
- repulsion (
\sim 48
meV) between
c
-electrons,

H_{W}
- repulsion (
\sim 47
meV) between
c
- and
f
-electrons.

As a widely adopted approximation in heavy fermion materials,
{\hat{H}}_{U_{2}}+{\hat{H}}_{V}+{\hat{H}}_{W}
can be decoupled in the Hartree channel due to the delocalized and localized natures of
c
- and
f
-electrons.
Hence these terms only effectively shift the band energies of
f
- and
c
-bands.
Then,
U_{1}
- the on-site repulsion of the
f
-electrons - is by far the largest energy scale of the problem - more than twice the hybridization (
\gamma
) and three times the exchange (
J
).
In Hartree-Fock (HF) calculations
U_{1}
is found to be the source of spontaneous symmetry-breakings.

Figure 2:
The self-consistent HF bands upon the ground states at the fillings
\nu=0,-1,-2,-3
. The color of the bands represent the contributing components, wherein yellow represents the
f
-electron states and blue represents the
c
-electron states.

Ground states —

Since
U_{1}
is much larger than the couplings (
\gamma
,
J
,
v_{\star}^{\prime}(\eta k_{x}\sigma_{x}+k_{y}\sigma_{y})
) between
f
- and
c
-electrons, a reasonable guess of the ground states would be product states of
f
-multiplets and the (gapless point) Fermi liquid state (
|\text{FS}\rangle
) of the half-filled
c
-electrons.
We call such product states “the parent states”.

E.g.
, the parent valley-polarized (VP) state at the charge neutrality (
\nu=0
) is

|\text{VP}^{\nu=0}_{0}\rangle=\prod_{\mathbf{R}}\prod_{\alpha=1,2}\prod_{s=\uparrow\downarrow}f_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,+,s}^{\dagger}|\text{FS}\rangle\ .

(8)

The parent Kramers inter-valley-coherent (K-IVC) state is a U(4)-rotation of
|\text{VP}^{\nu=0}_{0}\rangle
along the
\tau_{x}
-direction

\displaystyle|\text{K-IVC}^{\nu=0}_{0}\rangle=e^{-i\frac{\pi}{2}{\hat{\Sigma}}_{x0}}|\text{VP}^{\nu=0}_{0}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{\mathbf{R}}\prod_{s=\uparrow\downarrow}\frac{1}{2}(f_{\mathbf{R},1,+,s}^{\dagger}+f_{\mathbf{R},2,-,s}^{\dagger})(-f_{\mathbf{R},1,-,s}^{\dagger}+f_{\mathbf{R},2,+,s}^{\dagger})|\text{FS}\rangle\ .

(9)

Parent states at other integer fillings (
\nu=0,\pm 1,\pm 2,\pm 3
) can be similarly constructed
SM
.
They would be ground states of the Hamiltonian if
\gamma
,
J
,
v_{\star}^{\prime}
terms vanished;
hybridization of
f
- and
c
-electrons will develop,
i.e.
,
\langle f^{\dagger}c\rangle\neq 0
, otherwise.
The determination of ground states by self-consistent HF calculation with initial states given by the parent states is given in Ref.
SM
.
The numerically found HF ground states at the integer fillings (
fig.

2
) are fully consistent with those in Ref.
Lian et al. 2021
.

The parent states are so good initial states for the HF calculations that the one-shot HF is already qualitatively same as the self-consistent HF (see
fig.

3
).
Thanks to the simplicity of the heavy fermion model, the one-shot energies can be analytically estimated and we are able to derive two rules for the ground states
SM
.

First
, in the parent state,
f
-electrons at each site tend to be symmetric under permutation of U(4) indices to save the Coulomb energy (Hunds’ rule).
Both
eqs.

8
and
MATBG as Topological Heavy Fermion: I. Exact Mapping and Correlated Insulators
satisfy the first rule.

Second
, for U(4)-related states at a given integer filling
\nu
, the state that minimizes
{\hat{H}}_{M}+{\hat{H}}_{J}
is the ground state, where
{\hat{H}}_{M}
is the U(4)-breaking
M
term in
{\hat{H}}_{0}
(
eq.

1
).
This energy can be estimated by the lowest
\nu+4
levels of the mean field Hamiltonian
H^{(\Gamma_{1}\oplus\Gamma_{2})}
spanned by the
\Gamma_{1}\oplus\Gamma_{2}
basis of the
c
-bands at
\mathbf{k}=0
, which reads (up to constants)

H^{(\Gamma_{1}\oplus\Gamma_{2})}=M\sigma_{x}\tau_{0}{\varsigma}_{0}-\frac{J}{2}(\tau_{z}\overline{O}^{fT}\tau_{z}+\sigma_{z}\overline{O}^{fT}\sigma_{z})\ .

(10)

Here
\overline{O}^{f}_{\alpha\eta s,\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}=\langle f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}\rangle-\frac{1}{2}\delta_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\delta_{\eta\eta^{\prime}}\delta_{ss^{\prime}}
is the density matrix of the local
f
-orbitals with homogeneity assumed.
We have assumed that, for all the integer fillings, the eight lowest energy levels (closest to the gap) are always contributed by the
\Gamma_{1}\oplus\Gamma_{2}

c
-band basis - which are part of the flat bands - hence we only need to look at the
\Gamma_{1}\oplus\Gamma_{2}
subspace.
This assumption is fully justified by previous studies based on two-band projected Hamiltonian, where only
\Gamma_{1}\oplus\Gamma_{2}
basis exists, and will become clearer after we discuss the charge
\pm 1
excitations.

We now apply the second rule to
eq.

8
and (
MATBG as Topological Heavy Fermion: I. Exact Mapping and Correlated Insulators
) to determine the one-shot state of lowest energy.

\overline{O}^{f}
matrices given by
eqs.

8
and
MATBG as Topological Heavy Fermion: I. Exact Mapping and Correlated Insulators
are
\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{0}\tau_{z}{\varsigma}_{0}
and
-\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{y}\tau_{y}{\varsigma}_{0}
, respectively; the resulted lowest four levels of
H^{(\Gamma_{1}\oplus\Gamma_{2})}
are

\pm M-J/2
(each 2-fold) and
-\sqrt{M^{2}+J^{2}/4}
(4-fold), respectively.
It is direct to verify that the latter (K-IVC) has a lower energy.
Applying the two rules to parent states at other fillings, we obtain consistent results with the full numerical calculations in Refs.
Lian et al. 2021
;
Xie et al. 2021
.
We also obtain an analytical expression for the Chern numbers of ground states
SM
.

Figure 3:
The one-shot HF bands of the ground states at the fillings
\nu=0,-1
.
The red solid bands are the quasi-particle bands of the decoupled Hamiltonian, where
\gamma=v_{\star}^{\prime}=J=0
.
The horizontal and dispersive red bands are of the
f
- and
c
-electrons, respectively.
The touching point of the dispersive red bands at
\Gamma_{M}
is quadratic, while since
M
is small, it may look like linear.
The one-shot bands can be understood as a result of hybridization between
f
- and
c
-electrons.

Charge
\pm 1
excitations —

As shown in
figs.

2
and
3
and in Refs.
Bernevig et al. 2021b
;
Bultinck et al. 2020a
;
Vafek and Kang 2021
;
Cea and Guinea 2020
;
Zhang et al. 2020
;
Kang et al. 2021
, at
\mathbf{k}
away from
\Gamma_{M}
, the quasi-particle bands have a large gap (
\sim U_{1}
) and are relatively flat; at
\mathbf{k}
around
\Gamma_{M}
, the bands have significant dip. Such features are found related to the topology of the two flat-bands
Bernevig et al. 2021b
;
Vafek and Kang 2021
but have not yet been quantitatively understood.
The heavy fermion model provides a natural explanation to these features.
We first consider the decoupled limit (
\gamma=v_{\star}^{\prime}=J=0
) at
\nu=0
, where the
f
-electron bands are flat and have a (charge) gap
U_{1}
, and the
c
-electron bands are given by
H^{(c,\eta)}
(
fig.

3
(a)).
Tuning on
\gamma,v_{\star}^{\prime},J
then yields the one-shot quasi-particle bands.
At
\mathbf{k}=0
,
\gamma
gaps out the
\Gamma_{3}

c
-bands, and
J
further gaps out the
\Gamma_{1}\oplus\Gamma_{2}

c
-bands.
As the splitting of
\Gamma_{1}-\Gamma_{2}
is smaller than that of the
\Gamma_{3}
, the lowest excitations will carry
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}
representations, matching Refs.
Bernevig et al. 2021b
;
Vafek and Kang 2021
;
Bultinck et al. 2020a
and, according to the discussion after
eq.

10
, equals to
2\sqrt{M^{2}+J^{2}/4}
and
|J-2M|
for K-IVC and VP states, respectively.
At
\mathbf{k}\neq 0
, the
v_{\star}^{\prime}
term hybridizes the flat
f
-bands and dispersive
c
-bands.
For large
\mathbf{k}
, where the
c
-bands have very high kinetic energies, the hybridization is relatively weak and the gap is still approximately
U_{1}
.
Thus the shape of the quasi-particle bands is explained, and its bandwidth is approximately given by
(U_{1}-J)/2
when
M
is small.
As discussed in Ref.
SM
, the feature that the larger (
\sim U_{1}
) and smaller (
\sim J
) gaps are contributed by
f
- and
c
-electrons, respectively, is reflected in the STM spectra and Landau levels at different regions (AA or AB sites) of MATBG.

At nonzero fillings, the quasi-particle bands can also be understood as hybridized flat
f
-bands and dispersive
c
-bands, except that the
f
- and
c
-bands may feel different effective chemical potentials due to the density-density interactions between them.
For example, at
\nu=-1
, the upper branch of the
f
-bands is shifted to an energy close to the quadratic touching of the
c
-bands (
fig.

3
(b))
SM
.
Thus one of the hybridized bands is extremely flat.

Discussion —

The coexistence of quantum-dot-like behavior
Xie et al. 2019
;
Wong et al. 2020
and superconductivity
Cao et al. 2018b
;
Lu et al. 2019
;
Yankowitz et al. 2019
;
Saito et al. 2020
;
Stepanov et al. 2020
may now be understood - they come from two different types (
f
and
c
) of carriers.
In fact, inspired by the pomeranchuk effect experiments
Saito et al. 2021b
;
Rozen et al. 2021
and strange metal behavior
Cao et al. 2020
;
Polshyn et al. 2019
, authors of Refs.
Saito et al. 2021b
;
Rozen et al. 2021
;
Cha et al. 2021
also conjecture the possibility of coexistence of local momenta and itinerant electrons.
(The heavy fermion theory analog may also exist in other twisted materials
Ramires and Lado 2021
.)
Our paper
derives
and shows the exact mapping of MATBG to such a heavy-fermion type model.
As such, the machinery of heavy fermions
Si and Steglich 2010
;
Gegenwart et al. 2008
;
Coleman 1984
;
Dzero et al. 2010
;
Tsvelik and Wiegmann 1983
;
Werner et al. 2006
;
Lu et al. 2013
;
Weng et al. 2014
;
Kotliar et al. 2006
;
Emery and Kivelson 1992
;
Freyer et al. 2018
;
Furusaki and Nagaosa 1994
;
Cassanello and Fradkin 1996
;
Martin et al. 1997
;
Chakravarty and Rudnick 1995
can now be applied, for the first time, to MATBG.
We speculate that it will lead to pairing
Guinea and Walet 2018
;
You and Vishwanath 2019
;
Isobe et al. 2018
;
Liu et al. 2018
;
Gonzalez and Stauber 2019
;
Khalaf et al. 2021
;
Christos et al. 2020
;
König et al. 2020
;
Kennes et al. 2018
;
Huang et al. 2019
;
Guo et al. 2018
;
Chichinadze et al. 2020
;
Lian et al. 2019
;
Wu et al. 2018
;
Lewandowski et al. 2021
in nontrivial gap channels.

Supplementary Materials

S1
Bistritzer-MacDonald model

Here we briefly review the Bistritzer-MacDonald (BM) model
Bistritzer and MacDonald 2011
of magic-angle twisted bilayer graphene (MATBG).
Readers may refer to the supplementary materials of Ref.
Song et al. 2019
for more details.

S1.1
Basis and single-particle Hamiltonian

As shown in
fig.

S1
(a), the low energy states around the Dirac points at the K momenta of the two graphene layers form the moiré Brillouin zone (BZ) in the valley K. Similarly, the low energy states around the Dirac points at the K
′
momenta of the two layers form another moiré BZ in the valley K
′
.
Thus in the BM model there are two independent sets of basis from the two valleys.
We use the index
\eta
(
=+
for K and
-
for K
′
) to label the two graphene valleys.
We denote the basis as
c_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{Q},\alpha,\eta,s}
, where where
\mathbf{k}
is a momentum in the moiré Brillouin zone (BZ),
\mathbf{Q}
takes values in the lattice shown in
fig.

S1
(b),
\alpha=1,2
represents the graphene sublattice,
\eta=\pm
represents the graphene valley, and
s=\uparrow\downarrow
is the spin index.
There are two types of Q lattices: the blue lattice
\mathcal{Q}_{+}=\{\mathbf{q}_{2}+n_{1}\mathbf{b}_{M1}+n_{2}\mathbf{b}_{M2}\ |\ n_{1,2}\in\mathbb{Z}\}
and the red lattice
\mathcal{Q}_{-}=\{-\mathbf{q}_{2}+n_{1}\mathbf{b}_{M1}+n_{2}\mathbf{b}_{M2}\ |\ n_{1,2}\in\mathbb{Z}\}
, where
\mathbf{b}_{M1},\mathbf{b}_{M2}
are Moiré reciprocal lattice basis

\mathbf{b}_{M1}=\mathbf{q}_{2}-\mathbf{q}_{1},\qquad\mathbf{b}_{M_{2}}=\mathbf{q}_{3}-\mathbf{q}_{1}\ .

(S1)

For
\mathbf{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{+}
, the basis is defined as

(\mathbf{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{+})\qquad c_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{Q},\alpha,\eta,s}^{\dagger}=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{N_{\rm tot}}}\sum_{\mathbf{R}\in\mathrm{top}}e^{i(\mathbf{K}_{+}+\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{Q})\cdot(\mathbf{R}+\mathbf{t}_{\alpha})}c_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,s}^{\dagger}\qquad&\text{if}\;\eta=+\\
\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{N_{\rm tot}}}\sum_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\in\mathrm{bottom}}e^{i(-\mathbf{K}_{-}+\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{Q})\cdot(\mathbf{R}^{\prime}+\mathbf{t}_{\alpha}^{\prime})}c_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime},\alpha,s}^{\dagger}\qquad&\text{if}\;\eta=-\end{cases}\ ,

(S2)

and for
\mathbf{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{-}
, the basis is defined as

(\mathbf{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{-})\qquad c_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{Q},\alpha,\eta,s}^{\dagger}=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{N_{\rm tot}}}\sum_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\in\mathrm{bottom}}e^{i(\mathbf{K}_{-}+\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{Q})\cdot(\mathbf{R}^{\prime}+\mathbf{t}_{\alpha}^{\prime})}c_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime},\alpha,s}^{\dagger}\qquad&\text{if}\;\eta=+\\
\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{N_{\rm tot}}}\sum_{\mathbf{R}\in\mathrm{top}}e^{i(-\mathbf{K}_{+}+\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{Q})\cdot(\mathbf{R}+\mathbf{t}_{\alpha})}c_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,s}^{\dagger}\qquad&\text{if}\;\eta=-\end{cases}\ .

(S3)

Here
N_{\rm tot}
is the number of graphene unit cells in each layer,
\mathbf{R}
and
\mathbf{R}^{\prime}
indexes graphene lattices in the top and bottom layers, respectively,
\mathbf{t}_{\alpha}
and
\mathbf{t}_{\alpha}^{\prime}
are the sublattice vectors of the two layers, respectively,
\mathbf{K}_{+}
and
\mathbf{K}_{-}
are the K momentum of the top and bottom layers, respectively, and
c_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,s}
(
c_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime},\alpha,s}
) is the fermion operator with spin
s
at the atom site
\mathbf{R}+\mathbf{t}_{\alpha}
(
\mathbf{R}^{\prime}+\mathbf{t}_{\alpha}^{\prime}
).
The
\mathcal{Q}_{+}
(
\mathcal{Q}_{-}
) lattice is defined in such a way that
\eta\mathbf{K}_{+}+\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{Q}
(
\eta\mathbf{K}_{-}+\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{Q}
) with
\mathbf{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{+}
(
\mathbf{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{-}
) is the Dirac point position
\eta\mathbf{K}_{+}
(
\eta\mathbf{K}_{-}
) when
\mathbf{k}
equals to the high symmetry point
\eta K_{M}
(
\eta K_{M}^{\prime}
) of the moiré BZ, as sown in
fig.

S1
(b).

Figure S1:
The moiré BZ and the
\mathbf{Q}
lattice. (a) The blue and red hexagons represent the BZ’s of the top and bottom graphene layers, respectively. The blue and red dots represent the Diract points (K momentum) from the two layers. The vectors
\mathbf{q}_{1},\mathbf{q}_{2},\mathbf{q}_{3}
connecting the Dirac points of the two layers span the moiré BZ in the valley K (shaded with light yellow). Similarly, the Dirac points at the K
′
momentum form another moiré BZ in the valley K
′
(shaded with light orange). (b) We denote the blue and red
\mathbf{Q}
lattices as
\mathcal{Q}_{+}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{-}
respectively. In the BM model, the states in the valley K (K
′
) of the top layer and in the valley K
′
(K) of the bottom layer contribute to the lattice
\mathcal{Q}_{+}
(
\mathcal{Q}_{-}
).
(c) The unit cell in real space, where the maximal Wyckoff positions
1a
,
2c
,
3f
are marked by the black dots.

The BM model is given by

{\hat{H}}_{\rm BM}=\sum_{\eta s}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in{\rm MBZ}}\sum_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\sum_{\mathbf{Q},\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}}h_{\mathbf{Q}\alpha,\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}}^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{k})c_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{Q},\alpha,\eta,s}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{Q}^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime},\eta,s}

(S4)

where the single particle Hamiltonian reads

h_{\mathbf{Q}\alpha,\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}}^{(+)}(\mathbf{k})=v_{F}(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{Q})\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}\delta_{\mathbf{Q},\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}}+\sum_{j=1}^{3}[T_{j}]_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\delta_{\mathbf{Q},\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}\pm\mathbf{q}_{j}},\qquad h_{\mathbf{Q}\alpha,\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}}^{(-)}(\mathbf{k})=h_{-\mathbf{Q}\alpha,-\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}}^{(+)*}(-\mathbf{k}),

(S5)

T_{j}=w_{0}\sigma_{0}+w_{1}\sigma_{x}\cos\frac{2\pi(j-1)}{3}+w_{1}\sigma_{y}\sin\frac{2\pi(j-1)}{3}\ .

(S6)

Here
\mathbf{q}_{j}
are shown in
fig.

S1
, and
w_{0}
and
w_{1}
are the interlayer couplings in the AA-stacking and AB-stacking regions, respectively.
The length of
\mathbf{q}_{j}
is determined by the twist angle
\theta
,
i.e.
,
|\mathbf{q}_{j}|=k_{\theta}=2|\mathbf{K}|\sin\frac{\theta}{2}
.
In this work, we adopt the parameters
v_{F}=5.944\mathrm{eV\cdot\mathring{A}}
,
|\mathbf{K}|=1.703\mathrm{\mathring{A}^{-1}}
,
w_{1}=110\mathrm{meV}
,
\theta=1.05^{\circ}
.
The relation
h_{\mathbf{Q}\alpha,\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}}^{(-)}(\mathbf{k})=h_{-\mathbf{Q}\alpha,-\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}}^{(+)*}(-\mathbf{k})
is due to the time-reversal symmetry that transform the two valleys to each other.
The single-particle Hamiltonian (upon to a unitary transformation
Song et al. 2019
) is periodic with respect to the reciprocal lattice vectors
\mathbf{b}_{M1}=\mathbf{q}_{2}-\mathbf{q}_{1}=k_{\theta}(\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2},\frac{3}{2})
,
\mathbf{b}_{M2}=\mathbf{q}_{3}-\mathbf{q}_{1}=k_{\theta}(-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2},\frac{3}{2})
.
The moiré BZ and high symmetry momenta are defined in
fig.

S1
(b).
The real space unit cell is generated by
\mathbf{a}_{M1}=\frac{2\pi}{3k_{\theta}}(\sqrt{3},1)
,
\mathbf{a}_{M2}=\frac{2\pi}{3k_{\theta}}(-\sqrt{3},1)
, which satisfy
\mathbf{a}_{Mi}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{Mj}=2\pi\delta_{ij}
.
Area of the moiré unit cell is given by
\Omega_{0}=\mathbf{a}_{M1}\times\mathbf{a}_{M2}=\frac{8\pi^{2}}{3\sqrt{3}k_{\theta}^{2}}
.
Maximal Wyckoff positions in the unit cell are shown in
fig.

S1
(c).
The
1a
and
2c
positions correspond to the AA-stacking and AB-stacking regions, respectively.

We plot the band structures in the valley
\eta=+
for different
w_{0}
in
fig.

S2
.

The fact that the band structure is labeled by
\mathbf{k}
(in the moiré BZ) implies that
\mathbf{k}
labels the eigenvalues of translation operators of the moiré lattice.
We hence
define
the translation operator
T_{\mathbf{R}}
as

T_{\mathbf{R}}c_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{Q},\alpha,\eta,s}^{\dagger}T_{\mathbf{R}}^{-1}=e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{R}}c_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{Q},\alpha,\eta,s}^{\dagger}\ ,

(S7)

where
\mathbf{R}=n_{1}\mathbf{a}_{M1}+n_{2}\mathbf{a}_{M2}
, with
n_{1,2}\in\mathbb{Z}
, is a moiré lattice vector.
One should not confuse
T_{\mathbf{R}}
with the time-reversal symmetry (
T
) defined in
section

S1.2
.
We now verify
eq.

S7
at the commensurate twist angles, where
\mathbf{a}_{M1}
and
\mathbf{a}_{M2}
are integer linear combinations of the microscopic graphene lattice vectors
\mathbf{a}_{1},\mathbf{a}_{2}
.

T_{\mathbf{R}}
can be defined as translation acting on the atomic orbitals:
\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\to\mathbf{R}^{\prime}+\mathbf{R}
.
Then there is

\displaystyle T_{\mathbf{R}}c_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{Q},\alpha,\eta,s}^{\dagger}T_{\mathbf{R}}^{-1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N_{\rm tot}}}\sum_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\in l}e^{i(\eta\mathbf{K}_{l}+\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{Q})\cdot(\mathbf{R}^{\prime}+\mathbf{t}_{\alpha})}c_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}+\mathbf{R},\alpha,s}^{\dagger}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N_{\rm tot}}}\sum_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\in l}e^{-i(\eta\mathbf{K}_{l}+\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{Q})\cdot\mathbf{R}}e^{i(\eta\mathbf{K}_{l}+\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{Q})\cdot(\mathbf{R}^{\prime}+\mathbf{t}_{\alpha})}c_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime},\alpha,s}^{\dagger}\ ,

(S8)

where
l=\eta
(
-\eta
) for
\mathbf{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{+}
(
\mathcal{Q}_{-}
).
For commensurate angle,
\eta\mathbf{K}_{l}-\mathbf{Q}
is a moiré reciprocal lattice and hence
e^{-i(\eta\mathbf{K}_{l}+\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{Q})\cdot\mathbf{R}}=e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{R}}
.
Then
eq.

S7
is justified at commensurate angles.
We emphasize that
eq.

S7
is also well-defined even at non-commensurate angles.

Figure S2:
The band structure in the valley
\eta=+
of MATBG. In (a)-(f) the band structures with the inter-layer couplings
w_{0}/w_{1}
=1, 0.9, 0.8, 0.7, 0.6, 0.5 are plotted. The red crossings are energy eigenvalues of the BM model, and the black lines are bands of the effective model. The other parameters of the BM model are given by
v_{F}=5.944\mathrm{eV\cdot\mathring{A}}
,
|\mathbf{K}|=1.703\mathrm{\mathring{A}^{-1}}
,
w_{1}=110\mathrm{meV}
,
\theta=1.05^{\circ}
.
The parameters of the effective model for different
w_{0}/w_{1}
are given in
table

S4
.
We have set
v_{\star}^{\prime\prime}=B=C=0
for simplicity.

For later convenience, here we define our convention for Fourier transformation.
Let
N
,
\Omega_{0}
,
\Omega_{\rm tot}=N\Omega_{0}
be the number of moiré unit cells, area of each moiré unit cell, and the total area of the sample.
Then, for
\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}
in the moiré BZ and
\mathbf{Q},\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{l\eta}
with
l\eta=+
or
-
, integral over
\mathbf{r}
leads to the momentum conservation

\text{for}\quad\mathbf{Q},\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{l\eta}\quad\int d^{2}\mathbf{r}e^{(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime}-\mathbf{Q}+\mathbf{Q}^{\prime})\cdot\mathbf{r}}=\Omega_{\rm tot}\delta_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\delta_{\mathbf{Q},\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}}\ .

(S9)

The completeness relation of the plane waves is

\frac{1}{\Omega_{\rm tot}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}\sum_{\mathbf{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{l\eta}}e^{i(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{Q})\cdot\mathbf{r}}=\delta(\mathbf{r}),\qquad\text{for}\;l\eta=\pm.

(S10)

We introduce a continuous real space basis for the BM model

c_{l,\alpha,\eta,s}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{r})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\Omega_{\rm tot}}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in{\rm MBZ}}\sum_{\mathbf{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{l\eta}}e^{-i(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{Q})\cdot\mathbf{r}}c_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{Q},\alpha,\eta,s}^{\dagger}\ ,

(S11)

where
\Omega_{\rm tot}=N\Omega_{0}
is the total area of the sample in consideration,
N
is the number of Moiré unit cells,
\Omega_{0}
is the area of the Moiré unit cell,
l=\pm
is the layer index.
We can understand the
continuous
variable
\mathbf{r}
as the coarse-grained graphene lattice vector.
The graphene sublattice (
\alpha
) and valley (
\eta
) are now regarded as inner degrees of freedom.
At each
\mathbf{r}

c_{l,\alpha,\eta,s}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{r})
has sixteen components from two layers, two Graphene sublattice, two valleys, and two spins.
Unlike the usual real space basis, which just changes its position under a translation operation,
c_{l,\alpha,\eta,s}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{r})
also gains a phase factor under translation.
According to
eq.

S7
, there is

\displaystyle T_{\mathbf{R}}c_{l,\alpha,\eta,s}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{r})T_{\mathbf{R}}^{-1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\Omega_{\rm tot}}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in{\rm MBZ}}\sum_{\mathbf{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{l\eta}}e^{-i(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{Q})\cdot\mathbf{r}}T_{\mathbf{R}}c_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{Q},\alpha,\eta,s}^{\dagger}T_{\mathbf{R}}^{-1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\Omega_{\rm tot}}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in{\rm MBZ}}\sum_{\mathbf{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{l\eta}}e^{-i(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{Q})\cdot\mathbf{r}}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{R}}c_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{Q},\alpha,\eta,s}^{\dagger}\ .

(S12)

For convenience, we define the momenta

\Delta\mathbf{K}_{l}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{q}_{2},\qquad&l=+\\
-\mathbf{q}_{3},\qquad&l=-\end{cases}\ .

(S13)

Notice that
\eta\Delta\mathbf{K}_{l}-\mathbf{Q}
(
\mathbf{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\eta l}
) is a moiré reciprocal lattice and hence
e^{-i(\eta\Delta\mathbf{K}_{l}-\mathbf{Q})\cdot\mathbf{R}}=1
.
We hence have

T_{\mathbf{R}}c_{l,\alpha,\eta,s}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{r})T_{\mathbf{R}}^{-1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\Omega_{\rm tot}}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in{\rm MBZ}}\sum_{\mathbf{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{l\eta}}e^{-i\eta\Delta\mathbf{K}_{l}\cdot\mathbf{R}}e^{-i(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{Q})\cdot(\mathbf{r}+\mathbf{R})}c_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{Q},\alpha,\eta,s}^{\dagger}=e^{-i\eta\Delta\mathbf{K}_{l}\cdot\mathbf{R}}c_{l,\alpha,\eta,s}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{r}+\mathbf{R})\ .

(S14)

One may remove the factor
e^{i\eta\Delta\mathbf{K}_{l}\cdot\mathbf{R}}
by redefining the basis as
c_{l,\alpha,\eta,s}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{r})\to e^{i\eta\Delta\mathbf{K}_{l}\cdot\mathbf{r}}c_{l,\alpha,\eta,s}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{r})
.
However, this redefining will complicate the representation of crystalline symmetries (
section

S1.2
).
In this work we will stick to the definition
eq.

S11
of the real space basis.

S1.2
Discrete symmetries of the BM model

The single-particle Hamiltonian in each valley has the symmetry of the magnetic space group
P6^{\prime}2^{\prime}2
(# 177.151 in the BNS setting), which is generated by
C_{2z}T
,
C_{3z}
,
C_{2x}
and translation symmetries.
Since the two valleys are related by time-reversal symmetry
T
, the total system also has the
C_{2z}
symmetry (product of
C_{2z}T
and
T
).
The full crystalline symmetries of the two valleys form the space group
P622
(#177), which is generated by
C_{6z}
,
C_{2x}
, and translation symmetries.
We write the symmetry action on the fermion operators as

\hat{g}c_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{Q},\alpha,\eta,s}^{\dagger}\hat{g}^{-1}=\sum_{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}}c_{g\mathbf{k},\mathbf{Q}^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime},\eta^{\prime},s}^{\dagger}D_{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime},\mathbf{Q}\alpha\eta}(g)\ .

(S15)

The
D
matrices of the time-reversal symmetry and the single-valley symmetries are given by

D_{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime},\mathbf{Q}\alpha\eta}(T)=\delta_{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime},-\mathbf{Q}}[\sigma_{0}]_{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha}[\tau_{x}]_{\eta^{\prime}\eta},\qquad D_{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime},\mathbf{Q}\alpha\eta}(C_{3z})=\delta_{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime},C_{3z}\mathbf{Q}}[e^{i\frac{2\pi}{3}\sigma_{z}\tau_{z}}]_{\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime},\alpha\eta}

(S16)

D_{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime},\mathbf{Q}\alpha\eta}(C_{2x})=\delta_{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime},C_{2x}\mathbf{Q}}[\sigma_{x}]_{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha}[\tau_{0}]_{\eta^{\prime}\eta},\qquad D_{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime},\mathbf{Q}\alpha\eta}(C_{2z}T)=\delta_{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime},\mathbf{Q}}[\sigma_{x}]_{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha}[\tau_{0}]_{\eta^{\prime}\eta},

(S17)

where
\sigma_{x,y,z}
(
\sigma_{0}
),
\tau_{x,y,z}
(
\tau_{0}
) are Pauli (identity) matrices in the sublattice and valley spaces, and
\zeta_{\mathbf{Q}}=\pm 1
for
\mathbf{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\pm}
.

Besides the crystalline symmetries, the BM model also has a unitary particle-hole symmetry
P
that anti-commutes with the single-particle Hamiltonian.

P
transforms
\mathbf{k}
to
-\mathbf{k}
and the corresponding
D
matrix reads

D_{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime},\mathbf{Q}\alpha\eta}(P)=\zeta_{\mathbf{Q}}\delta_{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime},-\mathbf{Q}}[\sigma_{0}]_{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha}[\tau_{z}]_{\eta^{\prime}\eta}\ .

(S18)

(Unlike the
P
symmetry defined in Refs.
Bernevig et al. 2021a
;
Lian et al. 2021
;
Bernevig et al. 2021b
, where
P
is valley-independent, here
P
is chosen have opposite signs in the two valleys. The two definitions are equivalent because the Hamiltonian respects the valley-U(1) and one can redefine the particle-hole symmetry as
P\to\tau_{z}P
.)
In the first-quantized formalism the algebra between
P
and other symmetries are given by

P^{2}=-1,\qquad[P,T]=0,\qquad[P,C_{3z}]=0,\qquad\{P,C_{2x}\}=0,\qquad[P,C_{2z}T]=0\ .

(S19)

The particle-hole symmetry will be broken if the
\theta
-dependence of the single-layer Hamiltonian or quadratic terms in
\mathbf{k}
of the single-layer Hamiltonian are taken into account.

If, furthermore,
w_{0}=0
, the model acquires an effective chiral symmetry
C
that anti-commute with the single-particle Hamiltonian
Tarnopolsky et al. 2019
.

C
is referred to as the first chiral symmetry in Refs.
Song et al. 2021
;
Bernevig et al. 2021a
.

C
leaves
\mathbf{k}
invariant and its
D
matrix reads

D_{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime},\mathbf{Q}\alpha\eta}(C)=\delta_{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime},\mathbf{Q}}[\sigma_{z}]_{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha}[\tau_{0}]_{\eta^{\prime}\eta}\ .

(S20)

In the first-quantized formalism the algebra between
C
and other symmetries are given by

C^{2}=1,\qquad[C,T]=0,\qquad[C,C_{3z}]=0,\qquad\{C,C_{2x}\}=0,\qquad\{C,C_{2z}T\}=0,\qquad[C,P]=0\ .

(S21)

In the end we derive the symmetry actions on the continuous real space basis.
For unitary or anti-unitary operators, there are

\hat{g}c_{l,\alpha,\eta,s}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{r})\hat{g}^{-1}=\sum_{l^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}}c_{l^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime},\eta^{\prime},s}^{\dagger}(g\mathbf{r})D_{l^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime},l\alpha\eta}(g)\ .

(S22)

Notice that
P\mathbf{r}=-\mathbf{r}
.
(
P
is an inversion-like operation that interchanges the two layers but leave the sublattice invariant.)
Using the
D
matrices in momentum space and the definition of
c_{l,\alpha,\eta,s}(\mathbf{r})
(
eq.

S11
), we can derive the
D
matrices for the real space continuous basis

D_{l^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime},l\alpha\eta}(T)=[\varrho_{0}]_{l^{\prime}l}[\sigma_{0}]_{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha}[\tau_{x}]_{\eta^{\prime}\eta},\qquad D_{l^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime},l\alpha\eta}(C_{3z})=[\varrho_{0}]_{l^{\prime}l}[e^{i\frac{2\pi}{3}\sigma_{z}\tau_{z}}]_{\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime},\alpha\eta}\ ,

(S23)

D_{l^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime},l\alpha\eta}(C_{2x})=[\varrho_{x}]_{l^{\prime}l}[\sigma_{x}]_{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha}[\tau_{0}]_{\eta^{\prime}\eta},\qquad D_{l^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime},l\alpha\eta}(C_{2z}T)=[\varrho_{0}]_{l^{\prime}l}[\sigma_{x}]_{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha}[\tau_{0}]_{\eta^{\prime}\eta}\ ,

(S24)

D_{l^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime},l\alpha\eta}(P)=[-i\varrho_{y}]_{l^{\prime},l}[\sigma_{0}]_{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha}[\tau_{0}]_{\eta^{\prime}\eta},\qquad D_{l^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime},l\alpha\eta}(C)=[\varrho_{0}]_{l^{\prime},l}[\sigma_{z}]_{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha}[\tau_{0}]_{\eta^{\prime}\eta}\ .

(S25)

Here
\sigma_{x,y,z}
(
\sigma_{0}
),
\tau_{x,y,z}
(
\tau_{0}
),
\varrho_{x,y,z}
(
\varrho_{0}
) are Pauli (identity) matrices in the space of sublattice, valley, and layer, respectively.
We emphasize that, unlike the momentum space basis where
D(P)
is proportional to
\tau_{z}
, the real space basis has
D
proportional to
\tau_{0}
.
Here we take the particle-hole symmetry as an example to show how the
D
matrices are derived.
By definition, there is

\displaystyle Pc_{l,\alpha,\eta,s}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{r})P^{-1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\Omega_{\rm tot}}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in{\rm MBZ}}\sum_{\mathbf{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{l\eta}}e^{-i(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{Q})\cdot\mathbf{r}}Pc_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{Q},\alpha,\eta,s}^{\dagger}P^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{\Omega_{\rm tot}}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in{\rm MBZ}}\sum_{\mathbf{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{l\eta}}e^{-i(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{Q})\cdot\mathbf{r}}c_{-\mathbf{k},-\mathbf{Q},\alpha,\eta,s}^{\dagger}\ \eta\zeta_{\mathbf{Q}}

(S26)

We can replace
-\mathbf{Q}
by
\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}
with
\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{-l\eta}
.
Relabeling
\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{k}
as
\mathbf{Q}
and
-\mathbf{k}
, respectively, we obtain

\displaystyle Pc_{l,\alpha,\eta,s}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{r})P^{-1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\Omega_{\rm tot}}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in{\rm MBZ}}\sum_{\mathbf{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{-l\eta}}e^{-i(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{Q})\cdot(-\mathbf{r})}c_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{Q},\alpha,\eta,s}^{\dagger}\eta\zeta_{-\mathbf{Q}}

(S27)

Notice that, according to
eqs.

S2
and
S3
, for
\mathbf{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{-l\eta}
, the state
c_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{Q},\alpha,\eta,s}^{\dagger}
is in the layer
-l
, and the factor
\eta\zeta_{-\mathbf{Q}}
equals to
l
.
We hence obtain

Pc_{l,\alpha,\eta,s}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{r})P^{-1}=lc_{-l,\alpha,\eta,s}^{\dagger}(-\mathbf{r})\ ,

(S28)

consistent with
eq.

S25
.

For later convenience, we also derive the
D
matrices of
C_{2z}P
for the momentum space basis and real space basis as

D_{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime},\mathbf{Q}\alpha\eta}(C_{2z}P)=-i\zeta_{\mathbf{Q}}\delta_{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime},\mathbf{Q}}[\sigma_{x}]_{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha}[\tau_{y}]_{\eta^{\prime}\eta},\qquad D_{l^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime},l\alpha\eta}(C_{2z}P)=[-i\varrho_{y}]_{l^{\prime},l}[\sigma_{x}]_{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha}[\tau_{x}]_{\eta^{\prime}\eta}\ ,

(S29)

respectively.

S2
The single-particle Hamiltonian of the topological heavy fermion model

S2.1
Maximal localized Wannier functions for the local orbitals

We first summarize the representation analysis of the Wannier obstruction given in Ref.
Song et al. 2019
.
As proved in Ref.
Song et al. 2019
, due to the particle-hole symmetry, the middle two bands in each valley must form the irreducible co-representations (dubbed as irreps)

\Gamma_{1}\oplus\Gamma_{2};\ M_{1}\oplus M_{2};\ K_{2}K_{3}

(S30)

of the magnetic space group
P6^{\prime}2^{\prime}2
as long as they are gapped from higher and lower energy bands.
One can find the definitions of the irreps in
table

S1
or using the
Corepresentations
toolkit on the Bilbao Crystallographic Server
Elcoro et al. 2020
;
Xu et al. 2020
.
In
table

S2
we tabulate all the elementary band representations (EBRs),
i.e.
, the minimal local orbitals, and their corresponding irreps in momentum space.
One can also find the definition of EBRs using the
MBANDREP
toolkit on the Bilbao Crystallographic Server
Elcoro et al. 2020
;
Xu et al. 2020
.
There are only six allowed two-band band representations,
i.e.
,
2[A_{1}]_{a}\uparrow G
,
2[A_{2}]_{a}\uparrow G
,
[A_{1}\oplus A_{2}]_{a}\uparrow G
,
[E]_{a}\uparrow G
,
[A_{1}]_{c}\uparrow G
, and
[A_{2}]_{c}\uparrow G
, while none of them has the irreps in
eq.

S30
.
(The notation
[\rho]_{w}\uparrow G
represents the EBR of the magnetic space group
G
induced from the representation
\rho
at the Wyckoff possible
w
.)
Thus the middle two bands are not consistent with any local orbitals and must be topological.

, and

Table S2:
EBRs of the magnetic
space group
P6^{\prime}2^{\prime}2
(#177.151 in the BNS setting).
In the first row are the symbols and coordinates of Wyckoff positions.
In the second row are the corresponding site symmetry groups (magnetic point groups) and their unitary subgroups (point groups).
In the third row are the names of EBRs.
The notation
[\rho]_{w}\uparrow G
represents the EBR of the magnetic space group
G
induced from the irrep
\rho
at the Wyckoff possible
w
.
The orbitals that form the representation
[\rho]_{w}
are given in the fourth row.
The fifth row to seventh row give the irreps at high symmetry momenta of each EBR.

We now resolve the Wannier obstruction by adding higher energy bands in MATBG.
Both the first “passive” bands above the middle two bands and the first “passive” bands below the middle two bands form the degenerate irrep
\Gamma_{3}
at the
\Gamma_{M}
point.
If we hybridize the middle two bands with these higher energy bands, we can extract two topologically trivial bands that form the band representation (in terms of irreps)

[E]_{a}\uparrow G:\qquad\Gamma_{3};\ M_{1}\oplus M_{2};\ K_{2}K_{3}

(S31)

where
[E]_{a}
is formed by a
p_{x}
-like orbital and a
p_{y}
-like orbital at the
1a
position (triangular lattice).
In the remaining of this subsection we will discuss an explicit construction of the Wannier functions forming the representation
[E]_{a}
.
It has large overlaps (
fig.

S3
) with the lowest two bands for
\mathbf{k}
away from
\Gamma_{M}
— in most area of the moiré BZ excluding a small neighborhood of
\Gamma_{M}
, the lowest two bands of MATBG are almost completely spanned by the constructed Wannier functions.
While at (or around)
\Gamma_{M}
, the Wannier functions have zero (or small) overlap with the lowest two bands of MATBG.
Another set of basis, which form the representation
\Gamma_{1}\oplus\Gamma_{2}\oplus\Gamma_{3}
at
\Gamma_{M}
, will be introduced around
\Gamma_{M}
.
In
sections

S2.2
,
S2.3
,
S2.4
and
S2.5
we will write the single-particle Hamiltonian on the basis of the constructed Wannier functions and the remaining low-energy bands, which together form the representation
\Gamma_{1}\oplus\Gamma_{2}\oplus 2\Gamma_{3}
at the
\Gamma_{M}
point.

For simplicity in this subsection we will use the first-quantized formalism.
We define the basis

|\mathbf{k},\mathbf{Q},\alpha,\eta,s\rangle=c_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{Q},\alpha,\eta,s}^{\dagger}|0\rangle\ ,

(S32)

|\mathbf{r},l,\alpha,\eta,s\rangle=c_{l\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{r})|0\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\Omega_{\rm tot}}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in{\rm MBZ}}\sum_{\mathbf{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{l\eta}}e^{-i(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{Q})\cdot\mathbf{r}}|\mathbf{k},\mathbf{Q},\alpha,\eta,s\rangle\ .

(S33)

We write the trial Wannier functions as

|W^{\prime}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha=1,\eta,s}\rangle=\frac{1}{\lambda_{0}\sqrt{2\pi}}\sum_{l}\int d^{2}\mathbf{r}\ e^{i\frac{\pi}{4}l\eta-i\eta\Delta\mathbf{K}_{l}\cdot\mathbf{R}-(\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{R})^{2}/(2\lambda_{0}^{2})}|\mathbf{r},l,1,\eta,s\rangle

(S34)

|W^{\prime}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha=2,\eta,s}\rangle=\frac{1}{\lambda_{0}\sqrt{2\pi}}\sum_{l}\int d^{2}\mathbf{r}\ e^{-i\frac{\pi}{4}l\eta-i\eta\Delta\mathbf{K}_{l}\cdot\mathbf{R}-(\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{R})^{2}/(2\lambda_{0}^{2})}|\mathbf{r},l,2,\eta,s\rangle\ ,

(S35)

where
\lambda_{0}
represents the size of the Gaussian function and
\mathbf{R}
takes value in the triangular lattice sites, such that they form the representations

\displaystyle D^{f}(T)=\sigma_{0}\tau_{x},\quad D^{f}(C_{3z})=e^{i\frac{2\pi}{3}\sigma_{z}\tau_{z}},\quad D^{f}(C_{2x})=\sigma_{x}\tau_{0},\quad D^{f}(C_{2z}T)=\sigma_{x}\tau_{0},

\displaystyle D^{f}(P)=i\sigma_{z}\tau_{z},\quad D^{f}(C_{2z}P)=-i\sigma_{y}\tau_{y}\ ,

(S36)

same as
p_{x}\pm ip_{y}
at the
1a
Wyckoff position (per spin per valley).
Here
\sigma_{0,x,y,z}
,
\tau_{0,x,y,z}
are the identity or Pauli matrices in for the orbital (
\alpha=1,2
) and valley (
\eta=\pm
) degrees of freedom, respectively.
Since the Wannier functions transform diagonally (
i.e.
,
D^{f}(P)
in
eq.

S36
) under the
P
symmetry and
P
flips the layer (
eq.

S25
), each Wannier function should be a linear combination of states from the two layers.
The phase factors
e^{\pm i\frac{\pi}{4}l\eta}
in the Wannier functions are introduced to fix the gauge of the representation matrices
D^{f}
’s.
The phase factor
e^{-i\eta\Delta\mathbf{K}_{l}\cdot\mathbf{R}}
is required by the translation symmetry (
eq.

S14
).
One can verify that such defined trial Wannier functions satisfy

T_{\Delta\mathbf{R}}|W^{\prime}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\eta,s}\rangle=|W^{\prime}_{\mathbf{R}+\Delta\mathbf{R},\alpha,\eta,s}\rangle\ ,

(S37)

under a translation of the moiré lattice
\Delta\mathbf{R}
.
We emphasize that, for given
D^{f}(C_{3z})
, the sign of
D^{f}(P)
is
not
a gauge choice.
Since
[C_{3z},P]=0
,
P
and
C_{3z}
can be diagonalized at the same time and the
P
-eigenvalues in each
C_{3z}
eigen-space are determined.
For the trial Wannier functions, the
P
eigenvalue of the
C_{3z}
eigenstates with the eigenvalue
e^{i\frac{2\pi}{3}}
(
e^{-i\frac{2\pi}{3}}
) is
i
(
-i
).

We can express the trial Wannier functions on the plane-wave basis as

\displaystyle|W^{\prime}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha=1,\eta,s}\rangle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lambda_{0}\sqrt{2\pi\Omega_{\rm tot}}}\sum_{l=\pm}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in{\rm MBZ}}\sum_{\mathbf{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{l\eta}}\int d^{2}\mathbf{r}\ e^{i\frac{\pi}{4}l\eta-i\eta\Delta\mathbf{K}_{l}\cdot\mathbf{R}-(\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{R})^{2}/(2\lambda_{0}^{2})-i(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{Q})\cdot\mathbf{r}}|\mathbf{k},\mathbf{Q},1,\eta,s\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{2\pi\lambda_{0}^{2}}{\Omega_{\rm tot}}}\sum_{l=\pm}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in{\rm MBZ}}\sum_{\mathbf{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{l\eta}}e^{i\frac{\pi}{4}l\eta-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{R}-\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{0}^{2}(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{Q})^{2}}|\mathbf{k},\mathbf{Q},1,\eta,s\rangle

(S38)

|W^{\prime}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha=2,\eta,s}\rangle=\sqrt{\frac{2\pi\lambda_{0}^{2}}{\Omega_{\rm tot}}}\sum_{l=\pm}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in{\rm MBZ}}\sum_{\mathbf{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{l\eta}}e^{-i\frac{\pi}{4}l\eta-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{R}-\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{0}^{2}(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{Q})^{2}}|\mathbf{k},\mathbf{Q},2,\eta,s\rangle\ .

(S39)

We denote the energy eigenstates of the continuous BM model as
|\psi_{\mathbf{k},n,\eta,s}\rangle
.
The overlap matrix between the energy eigenstates and the trial Wannier functions are

A_{n,1}^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{k})=\langle\psi_{\mathbf{k},n,\eta,s}|W^{\prime}_{0,1,\eta,s}\rangle=\sqrt{\frac{2\pi\lambda_{0}^{2}}{\Omega_{\rm tot}}}\sum_{l=\pm}\sum_{\mathbf{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{l\eta}}e^{i\frac{\pi}{4}l\eta-\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{0}^{2}(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{Q})^{2}}\langle\psi_{\mathbf{k},n,\eta,s}|\mathbf{k},\mathbf{Q},1,\eta,s\rangle\ ,

(S40)

A_{n,2}^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{k})=\langle\psi_{\mathbf{k},n,\eta,s}|W^{\prime}_{0,2,\eta,s}\rangle=\sqrt{\frac{2\pi\lambda_{0}^{2}}{\Omega_{\rm tot}}}\sum_{l=\pm}\sum_{\mathbf{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{l\eta}}e^{-i\frac{\pi}{4}l\eta-\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{0}^{2}(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{Q})^{2}}\langle\psi_{\mathbf{k},n,\eta,s}|\mathbf{k},\mathbf{Q},2,\eta,s\rangle\ .

(S41)

We plot the
\sum_{\alpha}|A_{n,\alpha}(\mathbf{k})|^{2}
for each band
\epsilon_{\mathbf{k},n}
(
n=\pm 1,\pm 2,\pm 3
) in
fig.

S3
(a).
One can see that, as expected, at the
\Gamma_{M}
point, the overlap between the trial Wannier functions and the
\Gamma_{1}
,
\Gamma_{2}
states in the middle two bands are zero.

Figure S3:
Wannier functions for
w_{0}/w_{1}=0.8
.
In (a) the sizes of the red dots represent the overlaps between the trial Wannier functions and the Bloch states,
i.e.
,
\sum_{\alpha}|A_{n,\alpha}(\mathbf{k})|^{2}
, for the bands
\epsilon_{\mathbf{k},n}
with
n=\pm 1,\pm 2,\pm 3
.
(b) and (c) are absolute values of the two components
w_{+\beta,1}(\mathbf{r})
(
\beta=1,2
) of the constructed maximally localized Wannier functions.
The hexagons represent the moiré unit cell.
(d) The density of Wannier functions
i.e.
,
\sum_{l\beta}|w^{(\eta)}_{l\beta,\alpha}(\mathbf{r})|^{2}
, which does not depend on
\eta,\alpha
.
(e), (f), (g) are
|w_{+1,1}(\mathbf{r})|
,
|w_{+2,1}(\mathbf{r})|
, and the density of the approximate Wannier functions of the Gaussian form (
eqs.

S53
and
S54
), respectively.
The used parameters for the BM model are
v_{F}=5.944\mathrm{eV\cdot\mathring{A}}
,
|\Delta\mathbf{K}|=1.703\mathrm{\mathring{A}^{-1}}
,
w_{1}=110\mathrm{meV}
,
\theta=1.05^{\circ}
.

We feed the overlap matrix
A_{n,\alpha}^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{k})
(
n=\pm 1,\pm 2,\pm 3
,
\alpha=1,2
,
\eta=\pm
) into the machinery of Wannier90
Marzari and Vanderbilt 1997
;
Souza et al. 2001
;
Pizzi et al. 2020
to construct the maximally localized Wannier functions.
We have set
\lambda_{0}=0.1a_{M}
in practical calculations, with
a_{M}
being the moiré lattice constant.
We use a
18\times 18
momentum mesh and the energy window
\rm[-80meV,80meV]
for the disentanglement and Wannierization procedures.
Wannier90 returns
|W_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\eta,s}\rangle
on the plane-wave basis
|\mathbf{k},\mathbf{Q},\beta,\eta,s\rangle
as

|W_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\eta,s}\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{l=\pm}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in{\rm MBZ}}\sum_{\beta}\sum_{\mathbf{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{l\eta}}|\mathbf{k},\mathbf{Q},\beta,\eta,s\rangle e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{R}}\widetilde{v}^{(\eta)}_{\mathbf{Q}\beta,\alpha}(\mathbf{k})

(S42)

Here
N
is the number of Moiré unit cells.

\widetilde{v}^{(\eta)}_{\mathbf{Q}\beta,\alpha}(\mathbf{k})
returned by Wannier90 is originally stored on the basis of the Bloch wave functions of the energy bands.
Only the lowest six energy bands are involved in the Wannier functions because we have chosen the energy window
\rm[-80meV,80meV]
for the Wannierization, which only covers the lowest six bands (
fig.

S3
(a)).
We then transform
\widetilde{v}^{(\eta)}_{\mathbf{Q}\beta,\alpha}(\mathbf{k})
onto the basis
|\mathbf{k},\mathbf{Q},\beta,\eta,s\rangle
.
Since
|\mathbf{r},l,\beta,\eta,s\rangle
gains a phase factor
e^{-i\eta\Delta\mathbf{K}_{l}\cdot\mathbf{R}}
under the translation
\mathbf{R}
(
eq.

S14
), we can write
\langle\mathbf{r},l,\beta,\eta,s|W_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}\rangle
as

\langle\mathbf{r},l,\beta,\eta,s|W_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}\rangle=\langle\mathbf{r},l,\beta,\eta,s|T_{\mathbf{R}}|W_{0\alpha\eta s}\rangle=e^{-i\eta\Delta\mathbf{K}_{l}\cdot\mathbf{R}}\langle\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{R},l,\beta,\eta,s|W_{0\alpha\eta s}\rangle=e^{-i\eta\Delta\mathbf{K}_{l}\cdot\mathbf{R}}w_{l\beta,\alpha}^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{R})

(S43)

such that
w_{l\beta,\alpha}^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{R})=\langle\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{R},l,\beta,\eta,s|W_{0\alpha\eta s}\rangle
is a function of
\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{R}
.
The real space shape of the Wannier states can be calculated as

w_{l\beta,\alpha}^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{R})=e^{i\eta\Delta\mathbf{K}_{l}\cdot\mathbf{R}}\langle\mathbf{r},l,\beta,\eta,s|W_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N\Omega_{\rm tot}}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in{\rm MBZ}}\sum_{\mathbf{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{l\eta}}e^{i(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{Q})\cdot(\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{R})}\widetilde{v}^{(\eta)}_{\mathbf{Q}\beta,\alpha}(\mathbf{k})\ .

(S44)

Making use of
eqs.

S9
and
S10
, we obtain the inverse transformation

\widetilde{v}^{(\eta)}_{\mathbf{Q}\beta,\alpha}(\mathbf{k})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\Omega_{0}}}\int d^{2}\mathbf{r}\ w_{l_{\mathbf{Q},\eta}\beta,\alpha}^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{R})e^{-i(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{Q})\cdot(\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{R})}\ ,

(S45)

where
l_{\mathbf{Q},\eta}=\zeta_{\mathbf{Q}}\eta
is the layer the basis
|\mathbf{k},\mathbf{Q},\beta,\eta,s\rangle
belonging to and
\Omega_{0}=\Omega_{\rm tot}/N
is the area of the moiré unit cell.

We now determine the independent components of the Wannier function.
Due to the spin-SU(2) symmetry, the real space wave functions
w_{l\beta,\alpha}^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{r})
do not depend on spin.
Due to the time-reversal symmetry, the
w_{l\beta,\alpha}^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{r})
in the two valleys are just complex conjugation of each other,
i.e.
,

w_{l\beta,\alpha}^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{r})=w_{l\beta,\alpha}^{(-\eta)*}(\mathbf{r})\ .

(S46)

Due to the
P
symmetry (
\mathbf{r}\to-\mathbf{r}
), which has
D(P)=-i\varrho_{y}\sigma_{0}\tau_{0}
for the continuous real space basis (
eq.

S25
) and
D^{f}(P)=i\sigma_{z}\tau_{z}
for the Wannier functions (
eq.

S36
), the Wannier functions must satisfy the constraint

\sum_{l^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}}D_{l\alpha\eta,l^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}\eta}(P)w_{l^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime},\alpha}^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{r})=\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}}w_{l\alpha\eta,\alpha^{\prime}}^{(\eta)}(-\mathbf{r})D^{f}_{\eta\alpha^{\prime},\eta\alpha}(P)
,
i.e.
,

w_{-l\beta,\alpha}^{(\eta)}(-\mathbf{r})=il\eta(-1)^{\alpha}w_{l\beta,\alpha}^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{r})

(S47)

Due to the
C_{2z}T
symmetry, which has
D(C_{2z}T)=\varrho_{0}\sigma_{x}\tau_{0}
for the continuous basis (
eq.

S24
) and
D^{f}(C_{2z}T)=\sigma_{x}\tau_{0}
for the Wannier functions (
eq.

S36
), the Wannier functions must satisfy the constraint

w_{l\beta,\alpha}^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{r})=w_{l\overline{\beta},\overline{\alpha}}^{(\eta)*}(-\mathbf{r})

(S48)

where
\overline{\alpha}=2,1
for
\alpha=1,2
.
The above two conditions together imply a constraint (given by
PC_{2z}T
) that holds at each point
\mathbf{r}

w_{l\beta,\alpha}^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{r})=-il\eta(-1)^{\alpha}w_{-l\overline{\beta},\overline{\alpha}}^{(\eta)*}(\mathbf{r})\ .

(S49)

Due to the above three independent constraints, only two of the sixteen components in the real space functions
w_{l\beta,\alpha}^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{r})
are independent.
(Notice that each constraint reduce to half the number independent components.)
Without loss of generality, we will focus on the two independent components
w_{+\beta,1}^{(+)}(\mathbf{r})
(
\beta=1,2
).
We plot the absolute values of the two components
w_{+\beta,1}^{(+)}(\mathbf{r})
(
\beta=1,2
) in
fig.

S3
(b), (c), respectively.
Due to
eqs.

S49
and
S46
, the densities of the Wannier functions,
i.e.
,
\sum_{l\beta}|w_{l\beta,\alpha}^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{r})|^{2}
, do not depend on
\eta
and
\alpha
.
We plot the density of the Wannier functions in
fig.

S3
(d).
From the density plot we can see that the Wannier functions are very well localized.
To characterize the localization, we also calculate the square root of the spread of the Wannier functions,
i.e.
,

\lambda=\sqrt{\sum_{l\beta}\int d^{2}\mathbf{r}|w_{l\beta,\alpha}^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{r})|^{2}\mathbf{r}^{2}}\ .

(S50)

\lambda
does not depend on
\alpha
and
\eta
according to
eqs.

S49
and
S46
.
As tabulated in
table

S3
,
\lambda
ranges from
0.2965a_{M}
to
0.5330a_{M}
for
w_{0}/w_{1}
changing from 1.0 to 0.5, where
a_{M}
is the lattice constant of moiré unit cell.
The magnitude of the hopping between the constructed Wannier functions at nearest neighbor sites,
i.e.
,
t_{0}=|\langle W_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\eta,s}|H_{\rm BM}|W_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime},-\alpha,\eta,s}\rangle|
for
\mathbf{R}\mathbf{R}^{\prime}
being nearest neighbor pairs, is also extremely small.
(
\langle W_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\eta,s}|H_{\rm BM}|W_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime},\alpha,\eta,s}\rangle=0
due to the crystalline and
P
symmetries.)
As shown in
table

S4
, the
t_{0}
ranges from 0.01512meV to 0.3027meV for
w_{0}/w_{1}
changing from 1.0 to 0.5.
In the rest of this work we will omit the nearest neighbor hoppings.

w_{0}/w_{1}

(
a_{M}
)

(

)

(

)
