Table S3:
Parameters of Wannier functions for different
w_{0}/w_{1}
.

\alpha_{1,2}
and
\lambda_{1,2}
are parameters for the analytical form (
eqs.

S53
and
S54
) of the Wannier functions and they are fitted from the numerical Wannier functions.

\lambda
is the square root of the spread of the numerical Wannier functions.
The used parameters for the BM model are
v_{F}=5.944\mathrm{eV\cdot\mathring{A}}
,
|\Delta\mathbf{K}|=1.703\mathrm{\mathring{A}^{-1}}
,
w_{1}=110\mathrm{meV}
,
\theta=1.05^{\circ}
.

We find the Wannier functions shown in
fig.

S3
(b), (c) can be approximated by Gaussian functions of the form

w_{+1,1}^{(+)}(\mathbf{r})=\frac{\alpha_{1}}{\sqrt{2}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi\lambda_{1}^{2}}}e^{i\frac{\pi}{4}-\mathbf{r}^{2}/(2\lambda_{1}^{2})},\quad w_{+2,1}^{(+)}(\mathbf{r})=-\frac{\alpha_{2}}{\sqrt{2}}\frac{x+iy}{\lambda_{2}^{2}\sqrt{\pi}}e^{i\frac{\pi}{4}-\mathbf{r}^{2}/(2\lambda_{2}^{2})}\ .

(S51)

Even though the Wannier states transform as
p_{x}\pm ip_{y}
orbitals, which form the representation
[E]_{a}
at the
1a
position (
tables

S1
and
S2
), we see that the component
w_{+1,1}^{(+)}(\mathbf{r})
is
s
-orbital-like and the component
w_{+2,1}^{(+)}(\mathbf{r})
is (
p_{x}+ip_{y}
)-orbital-like.
The reason is that the inner degrees of freedom,
i.e.
, layer, sublattice, and valley, transform non-trivially under the crystalline symmetries.
For example, the layer, sublattice, and valley degrees of freedom transform under
C_{3z}
as
D(C_{3z})=\varrho_{0}e^{i\frac{2\pi}{3}\sigma_{z}\tau_{z}}
(
eq.

S23
), in order for the Wannier functions
w_{+\beta,1}^{(+)}(\mathbf{r})
to have the
C_{3z}
eigenvalue
e^{i\frac{2\pi}{3}}
(
eq.

S36
), there must be
e^{i\frac{2\pi}{3}[\sigma_{z}]_{\beta\beta}}w_{+\beta,1}^{(+)}(C_{3z}^{-1}\mathbf{r})=e^{i\frac{2\pi}{3}}w_{+\beta,1}^{(+)}(\mathbf{r})
.
The two components satisfy
w_{+1,1}^{(+)}(C_{3z}\mathbf{r})=w_{+1,1}^{(+)}(\mathbf{r})
and
w_{+2,1}^{(+)}(C_{3z}\mathbf{r})=e^{i\frac{2\pi}{3}}w_{+2,1}^{(+)}(\mathbf{r})
, respectively.
Thus they can be realized by the polynomials
1
(constant) and
x+iy
, respectively.
The phase factor
e^{i\frac{\pi}{4}}
is required by the symmetry
C_{2x}PC_{2z}T=C_{2y}PT
, whose action action on the continuous basis and representation on the Wannier states are
D(C_{2y}PT)=\varrho_{z}\sigma_{0}\tau_{0}
(
eqs.

S24
and
S25
) and
D^{f}(C_{2y}PT)=-i\sigma_{z}\tau_{z}
(
eq.

S36
), respectively.
Given
w_{+1,1}^{(+)}(\mathbf{r})\propto e^{-\mathbf{r}^{2}/(2\lambda_{1}^{2})}
,
w_{+1,1}^{(+)}(\mathbf{r})\propto(x+iy)e^{-\mathbf{r}^{2}/(2\lambda_{1}^{2})}
, the phases of the two components are constrained by the
C_{2y}PT
symmetry as

w_{+\beta,1}^{(+)*}(x,-y)=-iw_{+\beta,1}^{(+)}(x,y)\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad w_{+\beta,1}^{(+)}(\mathbf{r})=\pm e^{i\frac{\pi}{4}}|w_{+\beta,1}^{(+)}(\mathbf{r})|\ .

(S52)

However, the symmetry representation cannot fix the relative sign of the two components:
Suppose
w_{+\beta,1}^{(+)}(\mathbf{r})
forms the correct representation of
C_{2y}PT
, then
(-1)^{\beta-1}w_{+\beta,1}^{(+)}(\mathbf{r})
also forms the correct representation.
Thus there are two symmetry-allowed choices for the analytical expressions of
w_{+\beta,1}^{(+)}(\mathbf{r})
.
We have computed the overlaps between the two choices and the numerical Wannier functions and pick the choice (
eq.

S51
) that gives the larger overlap.

\lambda_{1}
can be fitted using the condition
|w_{+1,1}^{(+)}(\lambda_{1},0)|=e^{-\frac{1}{2}}|w_{+1,1}^{(+)}(0)|
, and
\lambda_{2}
can be fitted by the maximum of
|w_{+2,1}^{(+)}(x_{\max},0)|
, where
x_{\max}=\sqrt{2}\lambda_{2}
.

\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
are real coefficients satisfying
\alpha_{1}^{2}+\alpha_{2}^{2}=1
.
For
w_{0}/w_{1}=0.8
, we find
\lambda_{1}=0.1791a_{M}
,
\lambda_{2}=0.1850a_{M}
,
\alpha_{1}=0.7882
,
\alpha_{2}=0.6155
.
These parameters for other
w_{0}/w_{1}
values are tabulated in
table

S3
.
Using the constraints
eqs.

S46
,
S47
and
S48
, we obtain the other components as

w_{l1,1}^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{r})=\frac{\alpha_{1}}{\sqrt{2}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi\lambda_{1}^{2}}}e^{i\frac{\pi}{4}l\eta-\mathbf{r}^{2}/(2\lambda_{1}^{2})},\quad w_{l2,1}^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{r})=-l\frac{\alpha_{2}}{\sqrt{2}}\frac{x+i\eta y}{\lambda_{2}^{2}\sqrt{\pi}}e^{i\frac{\pi}{4}l\eta-\mathbf{r}^{2}/(2\lambda_{2}^{2})},

(S53)

w_{l1,2}^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{r})=l\frac{\alpha_{2}}{\sqrt{2}}\frac{x-i\eta y}{\lambda_{2}^{2}\sqrt{\pi}}e^{-i\frac{\pi}{4}l\eta-\mathbf{r}^{2}/(2\lambda_{2}^{2})},\qquad w_{l2,2}^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{r})=\frac{\alpha_{1}}{\sqrt{2}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi\lambda_{1}^{2}}}e^{-i\frac{\pi}{4}l\eta-\mathbf{r}^{2}/(2\lambda_{1}^{2})}\ .

(S54)

One can verify that the Wannier functions form the representations in
eq.

S36
.

We introduce the creation operators for the Wannier states as

f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}=\sum_{l\beta}\int d^{2}\mathbf{r}\ \langle\mathbf{r},l,\beta,s,\eta,s|W_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\eta,s}\rangle c_{l,\beta,\eta,s}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{r})=\sum_{l\beta}e^{-i\eta\Delta\mathbf{K}_{l}\cdot\mathbf{R}}\int d^{2}\mathbf{r}\ w_{l\beta,\alpha}^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{R})c_{l,\beta,\eta,s}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{r})\ .

(S55)

Under the translation
T_{\Delta\mathbf{R}}
the operator
f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}
transforms as

T_{\Delta\mathbf{R}}f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}T_{\Delta\mathbf{R}}^{-1}=f_{\mathbf{R}+\Delta\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}\ ,

(S56)

which follows
eq.

S37
directly.
Correspondingly, the momentum space operators can be defined as

f_{\mathbf{k}\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{R}}e^{i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{R}}f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}=\sum_{\mathbf{Q}\beta}c_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{Q},\beta,\eta,s}^{\dagger}\widetilde{v}^{(\eta)}_{\mathbf{Q}\beta,\alpha}(\mathbf{k})\ ,

(S57)

with
\widetilde{v}^{(\eta)}_{\mathbf{Q}\beta,\alpha}(\mathbf{k})
from
eq.

S45
.
Since
f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}
creates the Wannier states, they follows the same representation (
eq.

S36
) under symmetry operations

\hat{g}f_{\mathbf{k}\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}\hat{g}^{-1}=\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}}f_{g\mathbf{k}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s}^{\dagger}D^{f}_{\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime},\alpha\eta}(g),\qquad\hat{g}f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}\hat{g}^{-1}=\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}}f_{g\mathbf{R}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s}^{\dagger}D^{f}_{\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime},\alpha\eta}(g),

(S58)

with
D^{f}(g)
given by
eq.

S36
.

Hereafter, we refer to
f_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\eta,s}^{\dagger}
as
local orbitals
.
They transform as
p_{x}\pm ip_{y}
orbitals, which form the 2D irreducible representation
[E]_{a}
at the
1a
position (
tables

S1
and
S2
), under the crystalline symmetries.

S2.2
The topological conduction bands

As discussed in the last subsection, in the BM model the six low energy states per valley per spin form the representation
\Gamma_{1}\oplus\Gamma_{2}\oplus 2\Gamma_{3}
around the
\Gamma_{M}
point (
fig.

S2
), where
\Gamma_{1}\oplus\Gamma_{2}
are contributed by the middle two bands and
2\Gamma_{3}
are contributed by the higher energy bands.
The Wannier functions (in each valley-spin sector) constructed in the last subsection can span the middle two bands for momenta away from
\Gamma_{M}
, where the overlap with the flat bands is finite (
fig.

S3
(a)).
At the
\Gamma_{M}
point, the Wannier functions span a
\Gamma_{3}
representation.
Thus, in order to reproduce the correct band structure around the
\Gamma_{M}
point, we add four additional degree of freedom that form the representation
\Gamma_{1}\oplus\Gamma_{2}\oplus\Gamma_{3}
at
\Gamma_{M}
.
We denote the four states as
c_{\mathbf{k},a,\eta,s}^{\dagger}
(
a=1,2,3,4
).
Formally they can be written as

c_{\mathbf{k},a,\eta,s}^{\dagger}=\sum_{\mathbf{Q}\beta}\widetilde{u}^{(\eta)}_{\mathbf{Q}\beta,a}(\mathbf{k})c_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{Q},\beta,\eta,s}^{\dagger}\ ,

(S59)

where
\widetilde{u}^{(\eta)}_{\mathbf{Q}\beta,a}(\mathbf{k})
is to be determined.
As shown in next subsection,
c_{\mathbf{k},a,\eta,s}^{\dagger}
has a huge kinetic energy for large
\mathbf{k}
.
Therefore, in this work we will only consider
c_{\mathbf{k},a,\eta,s}^{\dagger}
within the cutoff
|\mathbf{k}|<\Lambda_{c}
.
Now we determine the coefficients
\widetilde{u}^{(\eta)}_{\mathbf{Q}\beta,a}(\mathbf{k})
.
We define the projector to the six lowest bands in the two valleys as

P_{\mathbf{Q}\alpha,\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}\beta}^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{k})=\sum_{n=\pm 1,\pm 2,\pm 3}u_{\mathbf{Q}\alpha,n}^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{k})u_{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}\beta,n}^{(\eta)*}(\mathbf{k}),

(S60)

where
u_{\mathbf{Q}\alpha,n}^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{k})
is the eigenstate of the BM Hamiltonian (
eq.

S5
) for the
|n|
-th band above (
n>0
) or below (
n<0
) the charge neutrality point.
We then define the projector to the two Wannier functions of the local orbitals as

Q_{\mathbf{Q}\alpha,\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}\beta}^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{k})=\sum_{\alpha=1,2}\widetilde{v}_{\mathbf{Q}\alpha,\alpha}^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{k})\widetilde{v}_{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}\beta,\alpha}^{(\eta)*}(\mathbf{k})\ .

(S61)

Since the two Wannier functions are constructed from the lowest six bands, there must be
P^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{k})Q^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{k})P^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{k})=Q^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{k})
.
This is guaranteed by the definition of our Wannier functions: The Bloch states of the Wannier functions
\widetilde{v}_{\mathbf{Q}\alpha,\alpha}^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{k})
are linear combinations of
u_{\mathbf{Q}\alpha,n}^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{k})
(
n=\pm 1,\pm 2,\pm 3
), as explained after
eq.

S42
.
The projector to the four low-energy states in the remaining bands is given by
P^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{k})-Q^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{k})
.
The states
\widetilde{u}^{(\eta)}_{\mathbf{Q}\beta,a}(\mathbf{k})
can be obtained as eigenstates of
P^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{k})-Q^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{k})
with the eigenvalue 1.

We emphasize that even though we have formally introduced
\widetilde{u}^{(\eta)}_{\mathbf{Q}\beta,a}(\mathbf{k})
, in practical calculations,
e.g.
,
section

S2.3
, we will always approximate
\widetilde{u}^{(\eta)}_{\mathbf{Q}\beta,a}(\mathbf{k})
by
\widetilde{u}^{(\eta)}_{\mathbf{Q}\beta,a}(0)
for low energy states, as the
c
-electrons are only relevant for a small
\mathbf{k}
region around
\Gamma_{M}
.

There is a gauge degree of freedom of
\widetilde{u}^{(\eta)}_{\mathbf{Q}\beta,a}(\mathbf{k})
.
We fix the gauge by requiring the representation matrices under the symmetry transformations

\hat{g}c_{\mathbf{k},a\eta s}^{\dagger}\hat{g}^{-1}=\sum_{a^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}}c_{g\mathbf{k},a^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s}^{\dagger}D^{c}_{a^{\prime}\eta^{\prime},a\eta}(g)

(S62)

to be

\displaystyle D^{c}(T)=\sigma_{0}\tau_{x}\oplus\sigma_{0}\tau_{x},\quad D^{c}(C_{3z})=e^{i\frac{2\pi}{3}\sigma_{z}\tau_{z}}\oplus\sigma_{0}\tau_{0},\quad D^{c}(C_{2x})=\sigma_{x}\tau_{0}\oplus\sigma_{x}\tau_{0},\quad D^{c}(C_{2z}T)=\sigma_{x}\tau_{0}\oplus\sigma_{x}\tau_{0}\ .

such that the
a=1,2
and
a=3,4
states form the
\Gamma_{3}
and
\Gamma_{1}\oplus\Gamma_{2}
representations at
\mathbf{k}=0
, respectively.
For example, the
C_{3z}
representation matrices of the
\Gamma_{3}
and
\Gamma_{1}\oplus\Gamma_{2}
states are given by
e^{i\frac{2\pi}{3}\sigma_{z}\tau_{z}}
and
\sigma_{0}\tau_{0}
, respectively.
Since the four states form three different representations at
\Gamma_{M}
, the states
\widetilde{u}^{(\eta)}_{\mathbf{Q}\beta,a}(\mathbf{k})
can be
uniquely
determined at
\mathbf{k}=0
.
However, for
\mathbf{k}\neq 0
, there is no easy method to uniquely fix the gauge of the four states.
Nevertheless, in practical calculations, we only need
\widetilde{u}^{(\eta)}_{\mathbf{Q}\beta,a}(0)
for the k
\cdot
p expansion.
Using the gauge fixed by
section

S2.2
and the algebra between
P
and the other symmetries (
eq.

S19
)

P^{2}=-1,\qquad[P,T]=[P,C_{3z}]=[P,C_{2z}T]=0,\qquad\{P,C_{2x}\}=0\ ,

(S64)

we can further determine the representation of
P
.
To satisfy
P^{2}=-1
and
[P,C_{2z}T]=0
, the representation of
P
in either the
\Gamma_{3}
or
\Gamma_{1}\oplus\Gamma_{2}
space must have the form
\pm i\sigma_{z}\tau_{0,z}
.
Representations involving
\tau_{x,y}
are not considered because
P
preserves the valley index.
The considered representations,
\pm i\sigma_{z}\tau_{0,z}
, already satisfy
\{P,C_{2x}\}=0
.
To commute with
T
, the only allowed form is
\pm i\sigma_{z}\tau_{z}
.
Numerically, we find the representation of
P
(and
C_{2z}P
for later use) as

D^{c}(P)=(-i\sigma_{z}\tau_{z})\oplus(-i\sigma_{z}\tau_{z}),\quad D^{c}(C_{2z}P)=(i\sigma_{y}\tau_{y})\oplus(i\sigma_{y}\tau_{y})\ .

(S65)

As explained after
eq.

S36
, for fixed
D^{c}(C_{3z})
, the sign of
D^{c}(P)
is
not
a gauge choice.
One may notice that the first two-by-two block of
D^{c}(P)
, which spans the
\Gamma_{3}
irrep, has an opposite sign with
D^{f}(P)
of the local
f
-orbitals (
eq.

S36
), which also span a
\Gamma_{3}
irrep.
Now we explain that they have to have opposite signs.
We denote the
\Gamma_{3}
irreps formed by the positive energy bands (
n=2,3
) and the negative energy bands (
n=-2,-3
) of the BM model as
\Gamma_{3+}
and
\Gamma_{3-}
, respectively.

\Gamma_{3+}
and
\Gamma_{3-}
transform into each other under
P
.
Thus, in the basis of
\Gamma_{3+}
and
\Gamma_{3-}
, in each eigen-subspace of
C_{3z}
, the
P
operator is off-diagonal.
To be explicit, we can write the representation matrices of
C_{3z}
and
P

\begin{pmatrix}e^{i\frac{2\pi}{3}}\sigma_{0}&0\\
0&e^{-i\frac{2\pi}{3}}\sigma_{0}\end{pmatrix},\qquad\begin{pmatrix}X&0\\
0&X^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}\ ,

(S66)

respectively.
The basis is ordered as: the first component of
\Gamma_{3+}
, the first component of
\Gamma_{3-}
, the second component of
\Gamma_{3+}
, the second component of
\Gamma_{3-}
, such that the first (second) two bases have the
C_{3z}
eigenvalue
e^{i\frac{2\pi}{3}}
(
e^{-i\frac{2\pi}{3}}
).

X
and
X^{\prime}
are off-diagonal such that
P
interchanges the two
\Gamma_{3}
representations.
After we diagonalize the
P
operator, the two
P
-eigenvalues in each eigen-subspace of
C_{3z}
in each valley must sum to zero.
Hence the sign of
D^{c}(P)
, which spans a diagonalized
\Gamma_{3}
, must be opposite to the sign of
D^{f}(P)
, which spans the other
\Gamma_{3}
.

Hereafter, we refer to
c_{\mathbf{k},a,\eta,s}^{\dagger}
(
a=1,2,3,4
) as
conduction bands
.

S2.3
Single-particle Hamiltonian of the topological conduction bands

In this subsection we use the first-quantized formalism for simplicity.
We can divide the single-particle Hamiltonian in the low energy space into four parts:

H^{(f,\eta)}(\mathbf{k})=Q^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{k})h^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{k})Q^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{k}),\qquad H^{(c,\eta)}(\mathbf{k})=(P^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{k})-Q^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{k}))h^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{k})(P^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{k})-Q^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{k}))\ ,

(S67)

H^{(fc,\eta)}(\mathbf{k})=Q^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{k})h^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{k})(P^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{k})-Q^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{k})),\qquad H^{(cf,\eta)}(\mathbf{k})=(P^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{k})-Q^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{k}))h^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{k})Q^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{k})\ ,

(S68)

where
h^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{k})
is the BM model (
eq.

S5
),
P^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{k})
(
eq.

S60
) and
Q^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{k})
(
eq.

S61
) are projectors to the lowest six bands (per spin valley) and the Wannier functions, respectively.
The first term is the single-particle Hamiltonian of the local orbitals, which almost vanishes (
\sim 0.15
meV for
w_{0}/w_{1}=0.8
) as discussed in
section

S2.1
and shown in
table

S4
.
The second term is the Hamiltonian of the conduction bands.
The third and fourth terms are the couplings between the local orbitals and the conduction bands.

We consider the k
\cdot
p expansion of the projected BM model
H^{(c,\eta)}(\mathbf{k})
around
\mathbf{k}=0
(
a,a^{\prime}=1\cdots 4
)

H^{(c,\eta)}_{aa^{\prime}}(\mathbf{k})=\langle\widetilde{u}_{a}^{(\eta,)}(0)|h^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{k})|\widetilde{u}_{a^{\prime}}^{(\eta)}(0)\rangle\ .

(S69)

We emphasize that the usual k
\cdot
p expansion
Winkler 2003
is made on the basis
\widetilde{u}^{(\eta)}_{\mathbf{Q}\beta,a}(0)
rather than
\widetilde{u}^{(\eta)}_{\mathbf{Q}\beta,a}(\mathbf{k})
.
This basis choice has two advantages: (i) It is exact if we keep an infinite number of bands (
a=1\cdots\infty
), (ii) It has simple
\mathbf{k}
-dependence.
We find that in our problem keeping four conduction bands is a good enough approximation.
Hereafter, we alway choose the Bloch states at
\mathbf{k}=0
as the basis whenever a k
\cdot
p expansion is needed.
Since
h^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{k})
has only zeroth order and linear order terms in
\mathbf{k}
,
H^{(c,\eta)}(\mathbf{k})
also only has constant terms and linear terms in
\mathbf{k}
.
As proved in the following paragraphs, to linear order terms of
\mathbf{k}
the effective Hamiltonian under the gauge
sections

S2.2
and
S65
has the form

H^{(c,+)}(\mathbf{k})=\begin{pmatrix}0&v_{\star}(k_{x}\sigma_{0}+ik_{y}\sigma_{z})\\
v_{\star}(k_{x}\sigma_{0}-ik_{y}\sigma_{z})&M\sigma_{x}\end{pmatrix}\ ,

(S70)

H^{(c,-)}(\mathbf{k})=H^{(c,+)*}(-\mathbf{k})\ ,

(S71)

where the
C_{3z}
eigenvalues of the four basis in the valley
\eta=+
are
e^{i\frac{2\pi}{3}}
,
e^{-i\frac{2\pi}{3}}
, 1, 1, respectively.
The parameters for different
w_{0}/w_{1}
obtained from
eq.

S69
are tabulated in
table

S4
.
Physical meaning of
M
is the splitting between the
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
states.

w_{0}/w_{1}

t_{0}

\gamma

M

v_{\star}

v_{\star}^{\prime}

v_{\star}^{\prime\prime}

1.0

0.01512

4.086

4.727

1.492

-0.01237

0.9

0.1099

-10.21

-0.01680

-24.75

-4.303

1.623

-0.03320

0.7

0.2276

-39.11

-4.483

-0.04012

0.6

0.2789

-52.91

-4.637
