Units

meV

\rm eV\cdot\mathring{A}

Table S4:
Parameters of the single-particle Hamiltonian of the topological heavy fermion model for different
w_{0}/w_{1}
.
Since
v_{\star}^{\prime\prime}
is extremely small compared to other parameters, in all the single-particle and many-body calculations we have set
v_{\star}^{\prime\prime}=0
.
Parameters of the BM model used to obtain this table are
v_{F}=5.944\mathrm{eV\cdot\mathring{A}}
,
|\mathbf{K}|=1.703\mathrm{\mathring{A}^{-1}}
,
w_{1}=110\mathrm{meV}
,
\theta=1.05^{\circ}
.

We now derive the form of
H^{(c,\eta)}
in the valley
\eta=+
to linear order of
\mathbf{k}
.
The effective Hamiltonian in the other valley can be obtained through the time-reversal symmetry,
i.e.
,
H^{(c,-)}(\mathbf{k})=H^{(c,+)*}(-\mathbf{k})
.
For simplicity, in the following we introduce an additional set of Pauli (identity) matrices
\zeta_{0,x,y,z}
to distinguish the
\Gamma_{3}
space (
a=1,2
) and the
\Gamma_{1}\oplus\Gamma_{2}
space (
a=3,4
).
The
a=1,2
and
a=3,4
span the subspaces of
\zeta_{z}=1
and
\zeta_{z}=-1
, respectively.
Due to
sections

S2.2
and
S65
, the symmetry operators in the valley
\eta=+
are given by

C_{3z}=e^{i\frac{2\pi}{3}\sigma_{z}}\oplus\sigma_{0},\qquad C_{2x}=\zeta_{0}\sigma_{x},\qquad C_{2z}T=\zeta_{0}\sigma_{x}K,\qquad P=-i\zeta_{0}\sigma_{z}\ ,

(S72)

where
\zeta_{0}\sigma_{x}\equiv\sigma_{x}\oplus\sigma_{x}
,
\zeta_{0}\sigma_{z}\equiv\sigma_{z}\oplus\sigma_{z}
.
We first look at the subspace
\Gamma_{3}
.
According to
C_{2z}T
, only
\sigma_{0,x,y}
terms are allowed in the Hamiltonian.
According to
P
,
\sigma_{0}
is odd in
k
while
\sigma_{x,y}
are even (constant).

\sigma_{0}
is invariant under
C_{3z}
, hence the coefficient before
\sigma_{0}
must be invariant under
C_{3z}
.
The lowest odd order polynomial satisfying this condition is in third order of
k
.
Thus the
\sigma_{0}
term vanishes to linear order of
k
.
Since
\sigma_{x,y}
are not invariant under
C_{3z}
, their (constant) coefficients must vanish to linear order of
\mathbf{k}
.
(One may show that the lowest order coefficients of
\sigma_{x,y}
terms are quadratic in
\mathbf{k}
.)

We then look at the subspace
\Gamma_{1}\oplus\Gamma_{2}
.
According to
C_{2z}T
, only
\sigma_{0,x,y}
terms are allowed.
According to
P
,
\sigma_{0}
is odd in
k
while
\sigma_{x,y}
are even (constant) in
k
.

\sigma_{0}
is invariant under
C_{3z}
, hence the coefficient before
\sigma_{0}
must be invariant under
C_{3z}
.
The lowest odd order polynomial satisfying this condition is in third order of
k
.
Thus the
\sigma_{0}
term vanishes to linear order of
k
.
In the subspace
\Gamma_{1}\oplus\Gamma_{2}
, both
\sigma_{x,y}
are invariant under
C_{3z}
, hence they are allowed by
C_{3z}
.
However
\sigma_{y}
is forbidden by the
C_{2x}
symmetry.
Thus the Hamiltonian in the
\Gamma_{1}\oplus\Gamma_{2}
subspace must have the form
M\sigma_{x}
.

Thirdly we look at the coupling between
\Gamma_{3}
and
\Gamma_{1}\oplus\Gamma_{2}
.
We only need to consider the off-diagonal terms
\zeta_{x,y}\sigma_{0,x,y,z}
.
According to
C_{2z}T
, only
\zeta_{x}\sigma_{0,x,y}
and
\zeta_{y}\sigma_{z}
terms are allowed.
According to
P
, the coefficients of
\zeta_{x}\sigma_{x,y}
are even (constant) in
k
while
\zeta_{x}\sigma_{0}
,
\zeta_{y}\sigma_{z}
are odd in
k
.
Since
\zeta_{x}\sigma_{x,y}
are not invariant under
C_{3z}
, their constant coefficients must vanish.
We then study the odd order terms
\zeta_{x}\sigma_{0}
,
\zeta_{y}\sigma_{z}
.
Under
C_{3z}
they transform as
C_{3z}\zeta_{x}\sigma_{0}C_{3z}^{-1}=\zeta_{x}\sigma_{0}\cos\frac{2\pi}{3}-\zeta_{y}\sigma_{z}\sin\frac{2\pi}{3}
,

C_{3z}\zeta_{y}\sigma_{z}\sigma_{0}C_{3z}^{-1}=\zeta_{y}\sigma_{z}\cos\frac{2\pi}{3}+\zeta_{x}\sigma_{0}\sin\frac{2\pi}{3}
.
Thus the
\zeta_{x}\sigma_{0},\zeta_{y}\sigma_{z}
terms can be chosen as
v_{\star}k_{x}\zeta_{x}\sigma_{0}-v_{\star}k_{y}\zeta_{y}\sigma_{z}
, which is also symmetric under
C_{2x}
.

In summary, the effective Hamiltonian
H^{(c,\eta)}
has the form in
eq.

S70
.

In Ref.
Song et al. 2021
the authors of the present work have shown that the single-particle Hamiltonian in each valley has a symmetry anomaly of the
C_{2z}T
and the particle-hole
P
symmetries.
The anomaly is reflected as unavoidable
4n+2
(
n\in\mathbb{N}
) Dirac cones at the Fermi-level.
Since the local orbitals are topologically trivial, the anomaly must be reflected in the band structure of the conduction bands, meaning that, if we turn off the coupling between local orbitals and conduction bands, the conduction bands (given by
eq.

S70
) must carry the symmetry anomaly by itself and hence be gapless.
The spectrum of
eq.

S70
is given by

\pm\frac{M}{2}\pm\sqrt{\frac{M^{2}}{4}+v_{\star}^{2}\mathbf{k}^{2}}

(S73)

which has a quadratic touching at
\mathbf{k}=0
.
To relate the quadratic touching to the symmetry anomaly, we consider adding a
C_{3z}
-breaking but
C_{2z}T
- and
P
-preserving term
D
into the Hamiltonian

\begin{pmatrix}D\sigma_{x}&v_{\star}(k_{x}\sigma_{0}+ik_{y}\sigma_{z})\\
v_{\star}(k_{x}\sigma_{0}-ik_{y}\sigma_{z})&M\sigma_{x}\end{pmatrix}\ .

(S74)

The quadratic touching is now split into two linear Dirac points at
k_{x}=\pm\frac{1}{v_{\star}}\sqrt{DM}
,
k_{y}=0
(
k_{x}=0
,
k_{y}=\pm\frac{1}{v_{\star}}\sqrt{DM}
) if
DM>0
(
DM<0
).
The two Dirac points have the same chirality because they are related by
P

Song et al. 2021
.
Thus the quadratic touching is topologically equivalent to two Dirac points with the same chirality.
According to the theorem proven in Ref.
Song et al. 2021
, the presence of
4n+2
(here
n=0
) Dirac points is an equivalent condition to the symmetry anomaly.

S2.4
Coupling between topological conduction bands and the local orbitals

We now consider the coupling between the conduction band states and the local orbitals

{\hat{H}}_{cf}=\sum_{\eta s}\sum_{a\alpha}\sum_{|\mathbf{k}|<\Lambda_{c}}\sum_{\mathbf{R}}\langle\mathbf{k}a\eta s|H|W_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}\rangle c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}+h.c.=\sum_{\eta s}\sum_{a\alpha}\sum_{|\mathbf{k}|<\Lambda_{c}}\sum_{\mathbf{R}}\langle\mathbf{k}a\eta s|H|W_{0\alpha\eta s}\rangle e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{R}}c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}+h.c.,

(S75)

where
|W_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}\rangle
(
\alpha=1,2
) and
|\mathbf{k}a\eta s\rangle=c_{\mathbf{k},a,\eta,s}^{\dagger}|0\rangle
(
a=1,2,3,4
) are the single particle states of the local orbitals and the conduction bands, respectively,
H
is the BM Hamiltonian (
eq.

S5
), and
\Lambda_{c}
is the cutoff for the conduction bands.
Since the Wannier functions of the local orbitals are localized, the integral
\langle\mathbf{k}a\eta s|H|W_{0\alpha\eta s}\rangle
must decay exponentially with
\mathbf{k}
.
However, the particular form of the decay is complicated due to two reasons.
First, as discussed in
section

S2.1
, the Wannier functions have two independent components (
eqs.

S53
and
S54
).
Thus the overlap between the conduction band states and the two components will decay differently.
Second, each conduction band state is not a simple plane-wave but a linear combination of plane-waves (
\mathbf{Q}
-vectors) with different wave-vectors (
eq.

S59
), hence the overlap for different plane-wave components will decay differently.
However, the role played by the decay is simply a truncation of the couplings between the local orbitals and high energy states.
We claim that the particular form of the truncation is not relevant in the low energy physics.
Thus, for simplicity here we assume that the overlap decays as
e^{-|\mathbf{k}|\lambda^{2}/2}
with
\lambda
being the square root of the spread of the Wannier function (
eq.

S50
).
We hence parameterize the integral as

\langle\mathbf{k}a\eta s|H|W_{0\alpha\eta s}\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}e^{-|\mathbf{k}|^{2}\lambda^{2}/2}H^{(cf,\eta)}_{a\alpha}(\mathbf{k})\ .

(S76)

Applying the Fourier transformation (
eq.

S57
) of the local orbitals, we can rewrite the coupling Hamiltonian as

{\hat{H}}_{cf}=\sum_{\eta s}\sum_{a\alpha}\sum_{|\mathbf{k}|<\Lambda_{c}}e^{-|\mathbf{k}|^{2}\lambda^{2}/2}H^{(cf,\eta)}_{a\alpha}(\mathbf{k})c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{k}\alpha\eta s}+h.c.

(S77)

For small
\mathbf{k}
, the factor
e^{-|\mathbf{k}|^{2}\lambda^{2}/2}
is negligible and we can obtain
H^{(cf,\eta)}_{a\alpha}(\mathbf{k})
as the k
\cdot
p Hamiltonian

H^{(cf,\eta)}_{a\alpha}(\mathbf{k})=\langle\widetilde{u}_{a}^{(\eta)}(0)|h^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{k})|\widetilde{v}_{\alpha}^{(\eta)}(0)\rangle,

(S78)

where
h^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{k})
is the BM model (
eq.

S5
),
|\widetilde{v}_{\alpha}^{(\eta)}(0)\rangle
are the Bloch state of the Wannier functions (
eq.

S42
), and
|\widetilde{u}_{a}^{(\eta)}(0)\rangle
are the Bloch states of the conduction bands (
eq.

S59
).
As explained after
eq.

S69
, we always choose
|\widetilde{v}_{\alpha}^{(\eta)}(0)\rangle
and
|\widetilde{u}_{a}^{(\eta)}(0)\rangle
rather than
|\widetilde{v}_{\alpha}^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{k})\rangle
and
|\widetilde{u}_{a}^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{k})\rangle
as the basis for the k
\cdot
p expansion.
Since
h^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{k})
only has zeroth and linear order terms in
\mathbf{k}
,
H^{(cf,\eta)}_{a\alpha}(\mathbf{k})
also only has constant and linear terms.
As proved in the next paragraph, we find the coupling Hamiltonian has the form

H^{(cf,\eta)}=\begin{pmatrix}\gamma\sigma_{0}+v_{\star}^{\prime}(\eta k_{x}\sigma_{x}+k_{y}\sigma_{y})\\
v_{\star}^{\prime\prime}(\eta k_{x}\sigma_{x}-k_{y}\sigma_{y})\end{pmatrix}\ .

(S79)

The first two-by-two block is the coupling between the local orbitals (forming
\Gamma_{3}
) and the
\Gamma_{3}
states of the conduction bands and hence allows a
\gamma\sigma_{0}
term, the second two-by-two block the coupling between the local orbitals and the
\Gamma_{1}\oplus\Gamma_{2}
states of the conduction bands and vanishes at
\mathbf{k}=0
.
The
\Gamma_{3}
representation carries angular momenta
L=\pm 1
and the
\Gamma_{1}\oplus\Gamma_{2}
representation carries the angular momentum
L=0
.
The above equation respects the angular momentum conservation up to
\Delta L=0
mod 3 because the system only has
C_{3z}
symmetry.
The parameters obtained from
eq.

S78
are tabulated in
table

S4
.
We find that
v_{\star}^{\prime\prime}
is very small compared to other velocities.
We hence will omit this term.

Here we give one more justification for the choice of the damping factor
e^{-|\mathbf{k}|^{2}\lambda^{2}/2}
.
First, around
\mathbf{k}=0
, the damping factor
e^{-|\mathbf{k}|^{2}\lambda^{2}/2}\sim 1
and works well because the
H^{(cf,\eta)}(\mathbf{k})
matrix is by construction (
eq.

S78
) accurate at
\mathbf{k}=0
.
Second, at large
\mathbf{k}
, the actual damping factor may be complicated due to the reasons given before
eq.

S76
, but the
f-c
coupling is weak compared to the energy separation of
f
- and
c
-bands.
Thus, the actual form of damping factor is not important.
Our approximation should have at least captured the main feature of the damping behavior - it assumes the Wannier function as a single exponential function and the conduction bands as monochromatic plane waves, which are reasonable due to the localized and delocalized natures of
f
- and
c
-electrons.
As will be discussed in
section

S2.5
and shown in
fig.

S2
, the choice

Now we use the symmetry principle to derive the form of
H^{(cf,+)}(\mathbf{k})
.

H^{(cf,-)}(\mathbf{k})
is given by
H^{(cf,+)*}(\mathbf{k})
due to the time-reversal symmetry.
We only keep zeroth and linear order terms in
\mathbf{k}
.
The symmetry operators on the single-particle states of the conduction bands (
c
) are given in
eq.

S72
.
According to
eq.

S36
, the symmetry operators on the single-particle states of the local orbitals (
f
) are

C_{3z}=e^{i\frac{2\pi}{3}\sigma_{z}},\qquad C_{2x}=\sigma_{x},\qquad C_{2z}T=\sigma_{x}K,\qquad P=i\sigma_{z}\ .

(S80)

We first look at the coupling between the
\Gamma_{3}
states (
a=1,2
) and the local orbitals.
According to
C_{2z}T
, only
\sigma_{0,x,y}
and
i\sigma_{z}
are allowed.
According to
P
, the Hamiltonian satisfy
(-i\sigma_{z})H^{(cf,\eta)}(-\mathbf{k})(i\sigma_{z})^{\dagger}=-H^{(cf,\eta)}(\mathbf{k})
, where
-i\sigma_{z}
and
i\sigma_{z}
are the representation matrix of
P
formed by the conduction bands and local orbitals, respectively.
Thus the
\sigma_{x,y}
terms must be odd in
k
and
\sigma_{0}
,
i\sigma_{z}
terms must be even in
k
.
The even order terms are constants to first order of
k
,
i.e.
,
\gamma\sigma_{0}+i\gamma^{\prime}\sigma_{z}
.

\gamma
respects both
C_{3z}
and
C_{2x}
symmetries.

\gamma^{\prime}
respects
C_{3z}
but breaks
C_{2x}
, hence the
\gamma^{\prime}
term is forbidden.
The two odd order terms
\sigma_{x,y}
transform under
C_{3z}
as
C_{3z}\sigma_{x}C_{3z}^{-1}=\sigma_{x}\cos\frac{2\pi}{3}+\sigma_{y}\sin\frac{2\pi}{3}
,
C_{3z}\sigma_{y}C_{3z}^{-1}=\sigma_{y}\cos\frac{2\pi}{3}-\sigma_{x}\sin\frac{2\pi}{3}
, thus the odd terms can be chosen as
v_{\star}^{\prime}(k_{x}\sigma_{x}+k_{y}\sigma_{y})
, which is also symmetric under
C_{2x}
.
Thus the coupling between the
\Gamma_{3}
states in the QDP and the local orbitals takes the form
\gamma\sigma_{0}+v_{\star}^{\prime}(k_{x}\sigma_{x}+k_{y}\sigma_{y})
.
We then consider couplings between the
\Gamma_{1}\oplus\Gamma_{2}
states (
a=3,4
) in the conduction bands and the local orbitals.
According to
C_{2z}T
, only
\sigma_{0,x,y}
and
i\sigma_{z}
are allowed.
According to
P
,
\sigma_{x,y}
terms must be odd in
k
and
\sigma_{0}
,
i\sigma_{z}
terms must be even in
k
.
The even order terms are constants to first order of
k
, however they break the
C_{3z}
symmetry as they couple states with angular momentum 0 to states with angular momenta
\pm 1
.
The two odd order terms
\sigma_{x,y}
transform under
C_{3z}
as
C_{3z}\sigma_{x}C_{3z}^{-1}=\sigma_{x}\cos\frac{2\pi}{3}-\sigma_{y}\sin\frac{2\pi}{3}
,
C_{3z}\sigma_{y}C_{3z}^{-1}=\sigma_{y}\cos\frac{2\pi}{3}+\sigma_{x}\sin\frac{2\pi}{3}
, thus the odd terms can be chosen as
v_{\star}^{\prime\prime}(k_{x}\sigma_{x}-k_{y}\sigma_{y})
, which is also symmetric under
C_{2x}
.

S2.5
Summary of the single-particle Hamiltonian

According to the discussions in
sections

S2.3
and
S2.4
, we can summarize the total single-particle Hamiltonian as

\displaystyle{\hat{H}}_{0}=

\displaystyle\sum_{\eta s}\sum_{aa^{\prime}}\sum_{|\mathbf{k}|<\Lambda_{c}}(H^{(c,\eta)}_{a,a^{\prime}}(\mathbf{k})-\mu\delta_{aa^{\prime}})c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k}a^{\prime}\eta s}-\mu\sum_{\eta s}\sum_{\mathbf{R}}f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{\eta s}\sum_{|\mathbf{k}|<\Lambda_{c}}\sum_{\mathbf{R}}\sum_{\alpha a}\left(e^{-|\mathbf{k}|^{2}\lambda^{2}/2-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{R}}H^{(cf,\eta)}_{a\alpha}(\mathbf{k})c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}+h.c.\right)\ .

(S81)

\mu
is the chemical potential,
\Lambda_{c}
is the cutoff of the momentum of the conduction bands,
H^{(c,\eta)}_{a,a^{\prime}}(\mathbf{k})
and
H^{(cf)}_{a\alpha}(\mathbf{k})
are given by

H^{(c,\eta)}(\mathbf{k})=\begin{pmatrix}0_{2\times 2}&v_{\star}(\eta k_{x}\sigma_{0}+ik_{y}\sigma_{z})\\
v_{\star}(\eta k_{x}\sigma_{0}-ik_{y}\sigma_{z})&M\sigma_{x}\end{pmatrix},

(S82)

H^{(cf,\eta)}=\begin{pmatrix}\gamma\sigma_{0}+v_{\star}^{\prime}(\eta k_{x}\sigma_{x}+k_{y}\sigma_{y})\\
0_{2\times 2}\end{pmatrix}\ ,

(S83)

respectively.
The parameters in the single-particle Hamiltonian are given in
table

S4
.

In principle, the cutoff
\Lambda_{c}
should be a small quantity compared to the Moiré BZ.
However, because the states at large
\mathbf{k}
’s have very high energies, we can formally extend
\Lambda_{c}
to infinity without changing the low energy physics.
A theoretical advantage of
\Lambda_{c}\to\infty
is that the yielded band structure will become periodic over the moiré BZ.
We label the momentum for the conduction bands as
\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{G}
with
\mathbf{k}
being in the moiré BZ and
\mathbf{G}
moiré reciprocal lattice.
Using a larger
\Lambda_{c}
implies a larger lattice of
\mathbf{G}
.
In
fig.

S2
we have plotted the band structures of
section

S2.5
using the cutoff
\Lambda_{c}=5\sqrt{3}k_{\theta}
.

As shown in Ref.
Bernevig et al. 2021a
, the
anti-unitary
single-particle symmetry
PC_{2z}T
(anti-commuting with single-particle Hamiltonian) implies a
unitary
many-body charge-conjugation symmetry
{\mathcal{P}}_{c}
that commutes with the Hamiltonian,
i.e.
,

{\mathcal{P}}_{c}f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}{\mathcal{P}}_{c}^{-1}=\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}}D^{f}_{\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime},\alpha\eta}(PC_{2z}T)f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s},\qquad{\mathcal{P}}_{c}c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s}^{\dagger}{\mathcal{P}}_{c}^{-1}=\sum_{a^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}}D^{c}_{a^{\prime}\eta^{\prime},a\eta}(PC_{2z}T)c_{-\mathbf{k}a^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s}\ ,

(S84)

where

D^{f}(C_{2z}TP)=-\sigma_{y}\tau_{z},\qquad D^{f}(C_{2z}TP)=(\sigma_{y}\tau_{z})\oplus(\sigma_{y}\tau_{z})\ .

(S85)

One can verify that
{\mathcal{P}}_{c}
commutes with
{\hat{H}}_{0}
in
section

S2.5
.

S2.6
The (first) chiral limit and the chiral U(4) symmetry

We find that when
v_{\star}^{\prime}=0
the Hamiltonian (
section

S2.5
)
anti-commute
with the
unitary
chiral operator

Cf_{\mathbf{k}\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}C^{-1}=\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}}f_{\mathbf{k}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s}^{\dagger}D^{f}_{\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime},a\eta}(C),\qquad Cc_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s}^{\dagger}C^{-1}=\sum_{a^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}}c_{\mathbf{k}a^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s}^{\dagger}D^{c}_{a^{\prime}\eta^{\prime},a\eta}(C)

(S86)

with

D^{f}(C)=\sigma_{z}\tau_{0},\qquad D^{c}(C)=(-\sigma_{z}\tau_{0})\oplus(\sigma_{z}\tau_{0})\ .

(S87)

According to
eqs.

S36
,
S2.2
and
S65
, we find that
C
satisfies the algebra at the single-particle level

C^{2}=1,\qquad[C,T]=0,\qquad[C,C_{3z}]=0,\qquad\{C,C_{2x}\}=0,\qquad\{C,C_{2z}T\}=0,\qquad[C,P]=0\ ,

(S88)

which are same as the first chiral symmetry discussed in
section

S1.2
(
eq.

S21
) and in Refs.
Song et al. 2021
;
Bernevig et al. 2021a
;
Tarnopolsky et al. 2019
;
Wang et al. 2021
.
Therefore, we identify
C
as the first chiral symmetry.
The band structure after imposing this chiral symmetry is shown in
fig.

S4
(b).

Refs.
Bernevig et al. 2021a
;
Bultinck et al. 2020a
;
Kang and Vafek 2019
have shown that the presence of chiral symmetry implies a so-called chiral non-flat U(4) symmetry of the projected interaction Hamiltonian.
(A similar U(4) symmetry was also found in Ref.
Kang and Vafek 2019
.)
Here we find that
C
indeed implies a U(4) symmetry of the single-particle Hamiltonian by using the symmetry
C_{2z}PC
.
In next paragraph we will show the chiral implied U(4) is consistent with the chiral non-flat U(4) discussed in Refs.
Bernevig et al. 2021a
;
Bultinck et al. 2020a
;
Kang and Vafek 2019
.
In
section

S3.6.2
we will show that the chiral U(4) symmetry is also an approximate symmetry of the interaction Hamiltonian.
Since the Hamiltonian anti-commutes with
P
and
C
, it must commute with the product
PC
as well as
C_{2z}PC
.
As discussed in
section

S1.2
,
C
is local in real space,
P
transforms
\mathbf{r}
to
-\mathbf{r}
, hence
C_{2z}PC
is also local in real space.
Its representation matrices are

D^{f}(C_{2z}PC)=\sigma_{x}\tau_{y},\qquad D^{c}(C_{2z}PC)=(\sigma_{x}\tau_{y})\oplus(-\sigma_{x}\tau_{y}).

(S89)

We introduce the continuous symmetry generator

{\hat{\Theta}}_{y0}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in\mathrm{MBZ}}\sum_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}\eta\eta^{\prime}s}[\sigma_{x}\tau_{y}]_{\alpha\eta,\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}}f_{\mathbf{k}\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{k}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{|\mathbf{k}|<\Lambda_{c}}\sum_{aa^{\prime}\eta\eta^{\prime}s}[(\sigma_{x}\tau_{y})\oplus(-\sigma_{x}\tau_{y})]_{a\eta,a^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}}c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k}a^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s}\ ,

(S90)

and it commutes with
H_{0}
.
Then we find that the Hamiltonian (
section

S2.5
) has the continuous symmetry

[e^{-i\theta{\hat{\Theta}}_{y0}},\hat{H}_{0}]=0\ .

(S91)

{\hat{\Theta}}_{y0}
together with the U(2)
\times
U(2) symmetry form the U(4) symmetry group.
The generators of the U(4) group can be explicitly written as

{\hat{\Theta}}_{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\eta\eta^{\prime}}\sum_{ss^{\prime}}\left(\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in\mathrm{MBZ}}\sum_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\Theta^{(\mu\nu,f)}_{\alpha\eta s,\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}f_{\mathbf{k}\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{k}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}+\sum_{|\mathbf{k}|<\Lambda_{c}}\sum_{aa^{\prime}}\Theta^{(\mu\nu,c)}_{a\eta s,a^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k}a^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}\right)\ ,

(S92)

where
\mu,\nu=0,x,y,z
and

\Theta^{(0\nu,f)}=\sigma_{0}\tau_{0}{\varsigma}_{\nu},\qquad\Theta^{(0\nu,c)}=(\sigma_{0}\tau_{0}{\varsigma}_{\nu})\oplus(\sigma_{0}\tau_{0}{\varsigma}_{\nu})\ ,

(S93)

\Theta^{(x\nu,f)}=\sigma_{x}\tau_{x}{\varsigma}_{\nu},\qquad\Theta^{(x\nu,c)}=(\sigma_{x}\tau_{x}{\varsigma}_{\nu})\oplus(-\sigma_{x}\tau_{x}{\varsigma}_{\nu})\ ,

(S94)

\Theta^{(y\nu,f)}=\sigma_{x}\tau_{y}{\varsigma}_{\nu},\qquad\Theta^{(y\nu,c)}=(\sigma_{x}\tau_{y}{\varsigma}_{\nu})\oplus(-\sigma_{x}\tau_{y}{\varsigma}_{\nu})\ ,

(S95)

\Theta^{(z\nu,f)}=\sigma_{0}\tau_{z}{\varsigma}_{\nu},\qquad\Theta^{(z\nu,c)}=(\sigma_{0}\tau_{z}{\varsigma}_{\nu})\oplus(\sigma_{0}\tau_{z}{\varsigma}_{\nu})\ .

(S96)

Here
\sigma_{\mu}
,
\tau_{\mu}
,
{\varsigma}_{\mu}
are Pauli matrices for the orbital, valley, and spin degree of freedom, respectively.
The first and second eight-by-eight blocks in
\Theta^{(\mu\nu,c)}
are for the
\Gamma_{3}
orbitals (
a=1,2
) and the
\Gamma_{1}\oplus\Gamma_{2}
(
a=3,4
), respectively.

Figure S4:
Band structures in the first, third chiral or flat limits.
(a) Band structure of the original topological heavy fermion model (
section

S2.5
) at
w_{0}/w_{1}=0.8
. (b) Band structure in the first chiral symmetry, where
v_{\star}^{\prime}
is imposed to zero.
(c) Band structure in the third chiral limit (flat limit), where
M
is imposed to zero.
(d) Band structure in the flat limit in the absence of the third chiral symmetry, where
M
is imposed to zero and a chiral breaking term
A
is taken into account. (See
section

S2.8
).
The other parameters are given in
table

S4
.

In the end we explicitly show that the U(4) symmetry in this chiral limit is consistent with the (first) chiral non-flat U(4) symmetry in Refs.
Bernevig et al. 2021a
;
Kang and Vafek 2019
;
Bultinck et al. 2020a
.
In the Appendix B2 of Ref.
Bernevig et al. 2021a
, the sewing matrices of
T
,
C_{2z}
, and
P
have been fixed as
\mathbf{k}
-independent matrices

B(T)=\sigma_{0}\tau_{x},\qquad B(C_{2z})=\sigma_{0}\tau_{x},\qquad B(P)=-i\sigma_{y}\tau_{z}\ .

(S97)

The generators of the (first) chiral non-flat U(4) symmetry in Ref.
Bernevig et al. 2021a
in this gauge are

\sigma_{0}\tau_{0}{\varsigma}_{\nu},\qquad\sigma_{0}\tau_{x}{\varsigma}_{\nu},\qquad\sigma_{0}\tau_{y}{\varsigma}_{\nu},\qquad\sigma_{0}\tau_{z}{\varsigma}_{\nu},\qquad(\nu=0,x,y,z)\ ,

(S98)

where
\sigma_{\mu}
,
\tau_{\mu}
, abd
{\varsigma}_{\mu}
are Pauli matrices for the band, valley, and spin degrees of freedom, respectively.
As shown in
section

S2.1
and
fig.

S3
, in most area of the moiré BZ, the middle two bands are mainly contributed by the local orbitals.
Thus we expect that, for
\mathbf{k}\neq 0
where the overlap between local orbitals and the middle two bands is finite, the U(4) generators on the local orbitals (after certain gauge transformation) should be same as those in
eq.

S98
.
We apply the gauge transformation
U=e^{i\frac{\pi}{4}\sigma_{0}\tau_{z}}e^{-i\frac{\pi}{4}\sigma_{x}\tau_{z}}
to the local orbitals such that the transformed representation matrices
UD^{f}(g)U^{\dagger}
become same as
eq.

S97
.
(See
eq.

S36
for definition of
D^{f}(g)
’s.)
The U(4) generators for the local orbitals in
eqs.

S93
,
S94
,
S95
and
S96
transform to

\sigma_{0}\tau_{0}{\varsigma}_{\nu},\qquad\sigma_{0}\tau_{y}{\varsigma}_{\nu},\qquad\sigma_{0}\tau_{x}{\varsigma}_{\nu},\qquad\sigma_{0}\tau_{z}{\varsigma}_{\nu},\qquad(\nu=0,x,y,z)\ .

(S99)

which are indeed same as
eq.

S98
.
For
\mathbf{k}=0
, our local orbital basis (forming the representation
\Gamma_{3}
) is different from the basis in Ref.
Bernevig et al. 2021a
(forming the representation
\Gamma_{1}\oplus\Gamma_{2}
), thus the above comparison is only valid for
\mathbf{k}\neq 0
.

S2.7
The flat limit and the flat U(4) symmetry

Refs.
Bernevig et al. 2021a
;
Bultinck et al. 2020a
discussed a flat limit where the kinetic energy of the flat middle two bands are discarded.
A so-called non-chiral flat U(4) symmetry emerges in this limit
Bernevig et al. 2021a
;
Bultinck et al. 2020a
.
We find that in the topological heavy fermion model we can achieve an exact flat band if we set
M=0
,
i.e.
, the
\Gamma_{1}-\Gamma_{2}
splitting is set to zero.
In the limit
M=0
, the Hamiltonian (
section

S2.5
)
anti-commutes
with the
unitary
chiral operator

Sf_{\mathbf{k}\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}S^{-1}=\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}}f_{\mathbf{k}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s}^{\dagger}D^{f}_{\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime},a\eta}(S),\qquad Sc_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s}^{\dagger}S^{-1}=\sum_{a^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}}c_{\mathbf{k}a^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s}^{\dagger}D^{c}_{a^{\prime}\eta^{\prime},a\eta}(S)

(S100)

with

D^{f}(S)=\sigma_{0}\tau_{0},\qquad D^{c}(S)=(-\sigma_{0}\tau_{0})\oplus(\sigma_{0}\tau_{0})\ .

(S101)

Since
S
is different from the first and second chiral symmetries discussed in Ref.
Song et al. 2021
;
Bernevig et al. 2021a
, we refer to
S
as the third chiral symmetry.
The local orbitals and the
a=3,4
conduction band basis have the chiral eigenvalue 1, whereas the
a=1,2
conduction band basis has the chiral eigenvalue
-1
.
Since only hoppings between opposite chiral eigenvalues are allowed (such that the Hamiltonian anti-commutes with
S
), the rank of the single-particle Hamiltonian at each
\mathbf{k}
(in a valley-spin sector) is at most 4.
Therefore, in this limit there must be two exact flat bands at the zero energy.
Correspondingly, we also refer to this chiral limit as the flat limit.
The band structure in this limit is given in
fig.

S4
(c).

Similar to the first chiral limit, the third chiral limit also implies a U(4) symmetry of the single-particle Hamiltonian.
In
section

S3.6.2
we will show that the flat U(4) symmetry is also an approximate symmetry of the interaction Hamiltonian.
Since the Hamiltonian anti-commutes with
P
and
S
, it must commute with the product
PS
as well as
C_{2z}PS
.

S
is local in real space, while
P
transforms
\mathbf{r}
to
-\mathbf{r}
, hence
C_{2z}PS
is also local in real space.
Its representation matrices are

D^{f}(C_{2z}PS)=-i\sigma_{y}\tau_{y},\qquad D^{c}(C_{2z}PS)=(-i\sigma_{y}\tau_{y})\oplus(i\sigma_{y}\tau_{y})\ .

(S102)

We introduce the continuous symmetry generator

{\hat{\Sigma}}_{y0}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in\mathrm{MBZ}}\sum_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}\eta\eta^{\prime}s}[\sigma_{y}\tau_{y}]_{\alpha\eta,\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}}f_{\mathbf{k}\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{k}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{|\mathbf{k}|<\Lambda_{c}}\sum_{aa^{\prime}\eta\eta^{\prime}s}[(\sigma_{y}\tau_{y})\oplus(-\sigma_{y}\tau_{y})]_{a\eta,a^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}}c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k}a^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s}\ ,

(S103)

and it commutes with
{\hat{H}}_{0}
.
Then we find that the Hamiltonian (
section

S2.5
) has the continuous symmetry

[e^{-i\theta{\hat{\Sigma}}_{y0}},\hat{H}_{0}]=0\ .

(S104)

Similar to the case in the first chiral symmetry, the above rotation together with the U(2)
\times
U(2) symmetry lead to a U(4) symmetry group.
The generators of the U(4) group can be explicitly written as

{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\eta\eta^{\prime}}\sum_{ss^{\prime}}\left(\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in\mathrm{MBZ}}\sum_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\Sigma^{(\mu\nu,f)}_{\alpha\eta s,\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}f_{\mathbf{k}\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{k}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}+\sum_{|\mathbf{k}|<\Lambda_{c}}\sum_{aa^{\prime}}\Sigma^{(\mu\nu,c)}_{a\eta s,a^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k}a^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}\right)\ ,

(S105)

where
\mu,\nu=0,x,y,z
and

\Sigma^{(0\nu,f)}=\sigma_{0}\tau_{0}{\varsigma}_{\nu},\qquad\Sigma^{(0\nu,c)}=(\sigma_{0}\tau_{0}{\varsigma}_{\nu})\oplus(\sigma_{0}\tau_{0}{\varsigma}_{\nu})\ ,

(S106)

\Sigma^{(x\nu,f)}=\sigma_{y}\tau_{x}{\varsigma}_{\nu},\qquad\Sigma^{(x\nu,c)}=(\sigma_{y}\tau_{x}{\varsigma}_{\nu})\oplus(-\sigma_{y}\tau_{x}{\varsigma}_{\nu})\ ,

(S107)

\Sigma^{(y\nu,f)}=\sigma_{y}\tau_{y}{\varsigma}_{\nu},\qquad\Sigma^{(y\nu,c)}=(\sigma_{y}\tau_{y}{\varsigma}_{\nu})\oplus(-\sigma_{y}\tau_{y}{\varsigma}_{\nu})\ ,

(S108)

\Sigma^{(z\nu,f)}=\sigma_{0}\tau_{z}{\varsigma}_{\nu},\qquad\Sigma^{(z\nu,c)}=(\sigma_{0}\tau_{z}{\varsigma}_{\nu})\oplus(\sigma_{0}\tau_{z}{\varsigma}_{\nu})\ .

(S109)

Here
\sigma_{\mu}
,
\tau_{\mu}
,
{\varsigma}_{\mu}
are Pauli matrices for the orbital, valley, and spin degree of freedom, respectively.
The first and second eight-by-eight blocks in
\Sigma^{(\mu\nu,c)}
are for the orbitals
a=1,2
and
a=3,4
, respectively.

Now we explicitly show that the U(4) symmetry in the third chiral limit (flat limit) of our heavy fermion model is consistent with the non-chiral flat U(4) symmetry in Ref.
Bernevig et al. 2021a
;
Bultinck et al. 2020a
, which commutes with the interaction Hamiltonian but anti-commutes with the single-particle BM Hamiltonian.
It is worth mentioning that our U(4) rotations, unlike the ones in Ref.
Bernevig et al. 2021a
, commutes with both the interaction Hamiltonian (approximately) and the single-particle Hamiltonian (if
M=0
).
In the Appendix B2 of Ref.
Bernevig et al. 2021a
, the sewing matrices of
T
,
C_{2z}
, and
P
have been fixed as
\mathbf{k}
-independent matrices shown in
eq.

S97
.
The generators of the non-chiral flat U(4) symmetry in this gauge are

\sigma_{0}\tau_{0}{\varsigma}_{\nu},\qquad\sigma_{y}\tau_{x}{\varsigma}_{\nu},\qquad\sigma_{y}\tau_{y}{\varsigma}_{\nu},\qquad\sigma_{0}\tau_{z}{\varsigma}_{\nu},\qquad(\nu=0,x,y,z)\ ,

(S110)

where
\sigma_{\mu}
,
\tau_{\mu}
, abd
{\varsigma}_{\mu}
are Pauli matrices for the band, valley, and spin degrees of freedom, respectively.
As shown in
section

S2.1
and
fig.

S3
, in most area of the moiré BZ, the middle two bands are mainly contributed by the local orbitals.
Thus we expect that, for
\mathbf{k}\neq 0
where the overlap between local orbitals and the middle two bands is finite, the U(4) generators on the local orbitals (after certain gauge transformation) should be same as those in
eq.

S110
.
We apply the gauge transformation
U=e^{i\frac{\pi}{4}\sigma_{0}\tau_{z}}e^{-i\frac{\pi}{4}\sigma_{x}\tau_{z}}
to the local orbitals such that the transformed representation matrices
UD^{f}(g)U^{\dagger}
become same as
eq.

S97
.
(See
eq.

S36
for definition of
D^{f}(g)
’s.)
The U(4) generators for the local orbitals in
eqs.

S106
,
S107
,
S108
and
S109
transform to

\sigma_{0}\tau_{0}{\varsigma}_{\nu},\qquad-\sigma_{y}\tau_{y}{\varsigma}_{\nu},\qquad\sigma_{y}\tau_{x}{\varsigma}_{\nu},\qquad\sigma_{0}\tau_{z}{\varsigma}_{\nu},\qquad(\nu=0,x,y,z)\ .

(S111)

which generate the same U(4) rotations as
eq.

S110
.
For
\mathbf{k}=0
, our local orbital basis (forming the representation
\Gamma_{3}
) is different from the basis in Ref.
Bernevig et al. 2021a
(forming the representation
\Gamma_{1}\oplus\Gamma_{2}
), thus the above comparison is only valid for
\mathbf{k}\neq 0
.

As will be revealed by the numerical calculations in
section

S4.2
, our flat-U(4) symmetry is a better approximation than our (first) chiral-U(4) symmetry.
A heuristic argument is the following.
The flat-U(4) breaking parameter
M
only affects low energy single-particle states of
H^{(c,\eta)}
(
eq.

S82
) because the
M
term is just a weak perturbation for high energy states, while the chiral-U(4) breaking parameter
v_{\star}^{\prime}
mainly affects high energy single-particle states because it enters the Hamiltonian in the form of
v_{\star}^{\prime}k_{x}\sigma_{0}\pm iv_{\star}^{\prime}k_{y}\sigma_{z}
.
Roughly speaking, in the presence of
v_{\star}^{\prime}
and
M
, low energy components of the single-particle Hamiltonian do not commute with the flat-U(4) rotation, while high energy components do not commute with the chiral-U(4) rotation.
Therefore, the energy change after a chiral-U(4) rotation will be larger than that after a flat-U(4) rotation, implying that the flat-U(4) symmetry is better approximation of the Hamiltonian.

In the end of this section we split the flat-U(4) generators to the fundamental representations of the flat-U(4) group.
In Ref.
Bernevig et al. 2021a
, the fundamental basis is obtained by diagonalizing the Pauli matrix
\sigma_{y}
in the band basis that is fixed by the sewing matrices in
eq.

S97
.
As discussed below
eq.

S110
, for
\mathbf{k}\neq 0
, the
f
-orbital basis is related to the basis of Ref.
Bernevig et al. 2021a
by the rotation
U=e^{i\frac{\pi}{4}\sigma_{0}\tau_{z}}e^{-i\frac{\pi}{4}\sigma_{x}\tau_{z}}
.
Thus now the fundamental U(4) representations should be obtained by diagonalizing
U^{\dagger}\sigma_{y}U=-\sigma_{z}\tau_{z}
.
In the following paragraph we prove that the eigenvalue
\xi
of
\sigma_{z}\tau_{z}
indeed labels fundamental U(4) representations.
It is worth mentioning that the choice of fundamental U(4) representations is not unique.
For example, since all the U(4) rotations in
eqs.

S106
,
S107
,
S108
and
S109
commute with
\sigma_{y}
, we can also use eigenvalues of
\sigma_{y}
in our heavy fermion basis to label fundamental representations.
We choose
\xi
because (i) it has the physical meaning of Chern number (
section

S4.5
)
Bernevig et al. 2021a
and (ii) it simplifies the exchange interaction (
section

S3.3
).

First, the flat-U(4) operators are already block-diagonal: They do not mix the eight
f
-orbitals, eight
\Gamma_{3}
basis of
c
-bands, and eight
\Gamma_{1}\oplus\Gamma_{2}
basis of
c
-bands with each other.
We need to further split each block into two fundamental representations of the flat-U(4) group.
We notice that the flat-U(4) generators either do not change the
\alpha,\eta
(
a,\eta
) indices of the
f
-electrons (
c
-electrons) or flip them at the same time.
Therefore, the index
\xi=(-1)^{\alpha-1}\eta
(
\xi=(-1)^{a-1}\eta
) of
f
-electrons (
c
-electrons) is unchanged under the flat-U(4) rotations.
(In general,
\xi
is not conserved by
{\hat{H}}_{0}
. For example, the
M
and
v_{\star}^{\prime}
terms in
eqs.

S82
and
S83
flip the
\xi
index.)
Consequently, the four components with the same
\xi
in each block form a fundamental representation of the flat-U(4) group.
For later convenience, we introduce the U(4) generator operators for fundamental representations separately

\displaystyle{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(f,\xi)}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\eta\eta^{\prime}}\sum_{ss^{\prime}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in\mathrm{MBZ}}\sum_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\delta_{\xi,(-1)^{\alpha-1}\eta}\delta_{\xi,(-1)^{\alpha^{\prime}-1}\eta^{\prime}}\Sigma^{(\mu\nu,f)}_{\alpha\eta s,\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}f_{\mathbf{k}\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{k}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}\ ,

(S112)

\displaystyle{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(c\prime,\xi)}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\eta\eta^{\prime}}\sum_{ss^{\prime}}\sum_{|\mathbf{k}|<\Lambda_{c}}\sum_{aa^{\prime}=1,2}\delta_{\xi,(-1)^{a-1}\eta}\delta_{\xi,(-1)^{a^{\prime}-1}\eta^{\prime}}\Sigma^{(\mu\nu,c)}_{a\eta s,a^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k}a^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}\ ,

(S113)

\displaystyle{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(c\prime\prime,\xi)}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\eta\eta^{\prime}}\sum_{ss^{\prime}}\sum_{|\mathbf{k}|<\Lambda_{c}}\sum_{aa^{\prime}=3,4}\delta_{\xi,(-1)^{a-1}\eta}\delta_{\xi,(-1)^{a^{\prime}-1}\eta^{\prime}}\Sigma^{(\mu\nu,c)}_{a\eta s,a^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k}a^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}\ ,

(S114)

respectively.
The total flat-U(4) generators are given by
{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}=\sum_{\xi}\left({\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(f,\xi)}+{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(c\prime,\xi)}+{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(c\prime\prime,\xi)}\right)
.

S2.8
Flat bands beyond the third chiral symmetry

Consider a system consisting of sublattices
A
and
B
, if only hoppings between
A
- and
B
-sublattices are allowed, then the system has a chiral symmetry, eigenvalues of which are
1
and
-1
for the two sublattices, respectively.
Then the Hamiltonian matrix
H(\mathbf{k})
satisfies

H_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}(\mathbf{k})=H_{\beta,\beta^{\prime}}(\mathbf{k})=0\ ,

(S115)

where
\alpha\in A
,
\beta\in B
.
We use
L_{A}
and
L_{B}
to represent the number of orbitals (per unit cell) in the
A
- and
B
-sublattices, respectively.
Without loss of generality, in the following we always label the sublattice with more (or equal number) orbitals as
A
such that
L_{A}\geq L_{B}
.
Because the dimension of the Hamiltonian matrix
H(\mathbf{k})
is
L_{A}+L_{B}
and the rank of the Hamiltonian is at most
2L_{B}
, which are contributed by
L_{B}
nonzero columns
H_{\alpha,\beta}(\mathbf{k})
and
L_{B}
nonzero rows
H_{\beta,\alpha}(\mathbf{k})
(
\alpha\in A
,
\beta\in B
),
H(\mathbf{k})
will have at least
L_{A}-L_{B}
zero-energy flat bands if
L_{A}>L_{B}
.
A recent study on flat bands based on the so-called
S
-matrix
Călugăru et al. 2021
pointed out that, in many cases, breaking the chiral symmetry does not necessarily destroy the flatness of flat bands.
A general condition for the presence of flat bands and their topological classifications are derived in Ref.
Călugăru et al. 2021
.
Here we only focus on a simple situation, where intra-sublattice hoppings in the smaller sublattice (
B
) are taken into account.
Adding such hoppings do break the chiral symmetry but do not change the rank of
H(\mathbf{k})
— with
H_{\beta,\beta^{\prime}}(\mathbf{k})
being nonzero,
H(\mathbf{k})
still has at most
L_{B}
nonzero columns and
L_{B}
nonzero rows.
Therefore, the flatness is robust against the hoppings within the smaller sublattice.

The above discussion explains the flat bands when we take into account certain chiral breaking terms in the heavy fermion Hamiltonian.
Following the symmetry argument in
section

S2.3
, we can add the quadratic term
A(k_{x}^{2}-k_{y}^{2})\sigma_{x}-2\eta Ak_{x}k_{y}\sigma_{y}
to the
a=1,2
block of
eq.

S82
.
This term commutes with
S
(
eq.

S100
) and hence breaks the third chiral symmetry.
However, it does not affect the flatness of the flat bands as long as
M=0
, as shown in
fig.

S4
(d), where
A=-30\mathrm{meV}/k_{\theta}^{2}
,
M=0
.
Now we explain the flatness in the presence of
A
using the
S
-matrix theory
Călugăru et al. 2021
.
In
section

S2.7
we have shown that the eigenvalues of
S
are
-1
,
+1
, and
+1
for the
\Gamma_{3}
conduction band basis (
a=1,2
),
\Gamma_{1}\oplus\Gamma_{2}
conduction band basis (
a=3,4
), and the local orbitals, respectively.
Before we add the
A
term, only hoppings between bases with opposite
S
eigenvalues are present.
We thus can label the
\Gamma_{3}
conduction band basis (
a=1,2
) and the local orbitals as the
A
-sublattice, and the
\Gamma_{1}\oplus\Gamma_{2}
conduction band basis (
a=3,4
) as the
B
-sublattice.
There is
L_{A}-L_{B}=2
and hence there will be two flat bands.
Since the
A
term is nothing but the hopping within the smaller (
B
) sublattice, according to the discussion in the last paragraph, the flatness of flat bands are stable against
A
.

S2.9
The U(4)
\times
U(4) symmetry in chiral flat limit

As discussed in Refs.
Bernevig et al. 2021a
;
Bultinck et al. 2020a
, the two-band projected interaction Hamiltonian will have a U(4)
\times
U(4) group when both the chiral and flat limits are achieved.
It should be emphasized that neither chiral-U(4) nor flat-U(4) is a factor U(4) group of the total U(4)
\times
U(4) group.
In the heavy fermion basis, when both limits are achieved (
M=v_{\star}^{\prime}=0
), the
\xi
index defined in
section

S2.7
, which equals
(-1)^{\alpha-1}\eta
and
(-1)^{a-1}\eta
for
f
- and
c
-electrons, respectively, and labels the fundamental flat-U(4) representations, is conserved in
{\hat{H}}_{0}
.
Moreover, every bilinear term in
{\hat{H}}_{0}
only involves a single
\xi
flavor, implying that we can write
{\hat{H}}_{0}
as
{\hat{H}}_{0}=\sum_{\xi}{\hat{H}}_{0}^{(\xi)}
where
{\hat{H}}_{0}^{(\xi)}
only contains fermion operators of the
\xi
flavor.
Then, not only the total flat-U(4) generators
{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}
, but also the generators acting in each
\xi
flavor,
i.e.
,
{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(\xi)}={\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(f,\xi)}+{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(c\prime,\xi)}+{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(c\prime\prime,\xi)}
(
eqs.

S112
,
S113
and
S114
), commute with
{\hat{H}}_{0}
because there is
[{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(\xi)},{\hat{H}}_{0}^{(\xi)}]=0
for the two
\xi
’s separately.
A generic rotation that is commuting with
{\hat{H}}_{0}
can be parameterized as

\exp\left(-i\sum_{\mu\nu\xi}\theta_{\mu\nu}^{(\xi)}{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(\xi)}\right)\ ,

(S116)

where
\theta_{\mu\nu}^{(+)}
can be different with
\theta_{\mu\nu}^{(-)}
. Therefore, when
M=v_{\star}^{\prime}=0
,
{\hat{H}}_{0}
has a U(4)
\times
U(4) symmetry group with the two factor U(4)’s generated by
{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(+)}
and
{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(-)}
, respectively.

{\hat{H}}_{0}

{\hat{H}}_{0}(M=0)

{\hat{H}}_{0}(v_{\star}^{\prime}=0)

{\hat{H}}_{0}(M=v_{\star}^{\prime}=0)

{\hat{H}}_{U}+{\hat{H}}_{V}+{\hat{H}}_{W}+{\hat{H}}_{J}+{\hat{H}}_{\widetilde{J}}

{\hat{H}}_{K}

Local Symmetry

U(2)
\times
U(2)

flat-U(4)

chiral-U(4)

U(4)
\times
U(4)

U(4)
\times
U(4)

chiral-U(4)

Table S5:
Summary of the local symmetries of the single-particle and interaction Hamiltonians. The single-particle Hamiltonian
{\hat{H}}_{0}
is summarized in
section

S2.5
, and interaction Hamiltonians
{\hat{H}}_{X}
(
X=U,V,W,J,\widetilde{J},K
) are summarized in
section

S3.6.1
.

Now we prove that, in general, when the single-particle or interaction Hamiltonian has both the chiral-U(4) and flat-U(4) symmetry, it must at least have the SU(4)
\times
SU(4) symmetry.
Since
\delta_{\xi,(-1)^{a-1}\eta}\delta_{\xi,(-1)^{a^{\prime}-1}\eta^{\prime}}
,
\sigma_{y}\tau_{x}
,
\sigma_{x}\tau_{y}
enter the coefficient matrices of
{\hat{\Sigma}}^{(\xi)}_{x\nu}
,
{\hat{\Sigma}}_{x\nu}
, and
{\hat{\Theta}}_{y\nu}
, respectively, and

\delta_{\xi,(-1)^{a-1}\eta}\delta_{\xi,(-1)^{a^{\prime}-1}\eta^{\prime}}[\sigma_{y}\tau_{x}]_{\alpha\eta,\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{2}[\sigma_{y}\tau_{x}+\xi\sigma_{x}\tau_{y}]

(S117)

we have

{\hat{\Sigma}}^{(\xi)}_{x\nu}=\frac{1}{2}{\hat{\Sigma}}_{x\nu}+\frac{\xi}{2}{\hat{\Theta}}_{y\nu}\ .

(S118)

Similarly, we have

{\hat{\Sigma}}^{(\xi)}_{y\nu}=\frac{1}{2}{\hat{\Sigma}}_{y\nu}+\frac{\xi}{2}{\hat{\Theta}}_{x\nu}\ .

(S119)

Therefore, when the Hamiltonian commutes with
{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}
and
{\hat{\Theta}}_{\mu\nu}
separately, it must also commute with
{\hat{\Sigma}}^{(\xi)}_{x\nu}
and
{\hat{\Sigma}}^{(\xi)}_{y\nu}
for
\xi=\pm
separately, which generate a SU(4)
\times
SU(4) group.
To see if the Hamiltonian has a higher U(4)
\times
U(4) symmetry, one has to explicitly check if the Hamiltonian also commute
{\hat{\Sigma}}^{(\xi)}_{00}
for
\xi=\pm
separately.
Since the Hamiltonian must respect charge-U(1),
i.e.
,
{\hat{\Sigma}}^{(+)}_{00}+{\hat{\Sigma}}^{(-)}_{00}
, one only need to check if
{\hat{\Sigma}}^{(+)}_{00}-{\hat{\Sigma}}^{(-)}_{00}
, or equivalently the index
\xi
, is conserved in the Hamiltonian.
In summary, the Hamiltonian will at least have a SU(4)
\times
SU(4) symmetry if it respects both flat-U(4) and chiral-U(4) symmetries.
Given the charge-U(1) is respected, the SU(4)
\times
SU(4) group will be promoted to U(4)
\times
U(4) if the Hamiltonian further commutes with
{\hat{\Sigma}}^{(+)}_{00}-{\hat{\Sigma}}^{(-)}_{00}
.

Using the criterion derived in the above paragraph, we find that most terms in the interaction Hamiltonian also has the U(4)
\times
U(4) symmetry (
section

S3.6.1
).
We summarize the local symmetries of all the single-particle and interaction Hamiltonians in
table

S5
.

S2.10
Fubini-Study metric and Berry’s curvature of the flat bands in the chiral flat limit

It has been shown that the flat (Chern) bands of the BM model in the chiral limit are analytically solvable
Tarnopolsky et al. 2019
.
Because the Chern band solutions are holomorphic functions of
k_{x}+ik_{y}
(or
k_{x}-ik_{y}
), there is a simple relation between the Berry’s curvature
\Omega(\mathbf{k})
and the Fubini-Study metric
g_{ij}(\mathbf{k})
,
i.e.
, ,
g_{ij}(\mathbf{k})=\frac{1}{2}|\Omega(\mathbf{k})|\delta_{ij}

Ledwith et al. 2020
, which further leads to
4\det g(\mathbf{k})=|\Omega(\mathbf{k})|^{2}
, also known as the ideal droplet condition.
The ideal droplet condition together with the flatness of Berry’s curvature,
i.e.
,
\Omega(\mathbf{k})=const.
, lead to the GMP algebra of density operators
Girvin et al. 1986
;
Parameswaran et al. 2013
;
Roy 2014
and hence can give rise to fractional Chern insulator phases in spin and valley polarized MATBG
Sheffer and Stern 2021
;
Repellin and Senthil 2020
;
Abouelkomsan et al. 2020
;
Ledwith et al. 2020
.

In this subsection we will show that in the chiral flat limit (
v_{\star}^{\prime}=M=0
) of our heavy fermion model the Chern band states are also analytically solvable and are holomorphic functions of
k_{x}+ik_{y}
or
k_{x}-ik_{y}
(up to a normalization constant).
Thus the ideal droplet condition of the flat band will be automatically satisfied.
(It is worth noting that this chiral limit is theoretically achieved by imposing
v_{\star}^{\prime}=M=0
.
It shares the same symmetries as the BM model in the chiral limit, where
w_{0}/w_{1}=0
.
But the other parameters,
e.g.
,
M
,
v_{\star}
, are not required to be obtained at
w_{0}/w_{1}=0
.)
According to
sections

S2.7
and
S2.6
, when both (first) chiral and flat limits are achieved the single-particle Hamiltonian must commute with
C\cdot S
, representation of which is given by

D^{f}(C\cdot S)=\sigma_{z}\tau_{0},\qquad D^{c}(C\cdot S)=\sigma_{z}\tau_{0}\oplus\sigma_{z}\tau_{0}\ .

(S120)

Therefore, we can block-diagonalize the Hamiltonian using the eigenvalues (
\zeta
) of
C\cdot S
.
The total Hamiltonian in momentum space can be rewritten as

\displaystyle{\hat{H}}_{0}=

\displaystyle\sum_{\eta s}\sum_{aa^{\prime}=1,3}\sum_{|\mathbf{k}|<\Lambda_{c}}H^{(c,\eta,+)}_{a,a^{\prime}}(\mathbf{k})c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k}a^{\prime}\eta s}+\sum_{\eta s}\sum_{|\mathbf{k}|<\Lambda_{c}}\sum_{a=1,3}\left(e^{-|\mathbf{k}|^{2}\lambda^{2}/2}H^{(cf,\eta,+)}_{a1}(\mathbf{k})c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{k}1\eta s}+h.c.\right)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum_{\eta s}\sum_{aa^{\prime}=2,4}\sum_{|\mathbf{k}|<\Lambda_{c}}H^{(c,\eta,-)}_{a,a^{\prime}}(\mathbf{k})c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k}a^{\prime}\eta s}+\sum_{\eta s}\sum_{|\mathbf{k}|<\Lambda_{c}}\sum_{a=2,4}\left(e^{-|\mathbf{k}|^{2}\lambda^{2}/2}H^{(cf,\eta,-)}_{a2}(\mathbf{k})c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{k}2\eta s}+h.c.\right)

(S121)

where the two-by-two matrix
H^{(c,\eta,\zeta)}
and two
H^{(cf,\eta,\zeta)}
(two-by-one) are given by

H^{(c,\eta,\zeta)}=v_{\star}\begin{pmatrix}0&\eta k_{x}+i\zeta k_{y}\\
\eta k_{x}-i\zeta k_{y}&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad H^{(cf,\eta,\zeta)}=\begin{pmatrix}\gamma\\
0\end{pmatrix}\ .

(S122)

The Hamiltonian in each
\zeta
sector still anti-commutes with
S
and hence must have flat bands (
section

S2.7
).
As explained in
section

S2.5
, for the band structure to be periodic in MBZ, we can formally extend the cutoff of
c
-bands,
\Lambda_{c}
, to infinity.
Here we just assume
\Lambda_{c}
is much larger than the size of MBZ.
One must be aware that, for a moire reciprocal lattice
\mathbf{G}
, there is
c_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{G},a\eta s}\neq c_{\mathbf{k},a\eta s}
and
f_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{G},\alpha\eta s}=f_{\mathbf{k},\alpha\eta s}
because the former is a continuous field while the second is a lattice.
Then the flat band solution is in the sector
(\zeta,\eta,s)
can be written as

d_{\mathbf{k},\zeta=+,\eta,s}^{\dagger}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}_{\mathbf{k}}}}f_{\mathbf{k},1,\eta,s}^{\dagger}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}_{\mathbf{k}}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{G}\\
|\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{G}|<\Lambda_{c}\end{subarray}}\frac{\gamma/v_{\star}}{\eta(k_{x}+G_{x})+i(k_{y}+G_{y})}c_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{G},3,\eta,s}^{\dagger}\ ,

(S123)

d_{\mathbf{k},\zeta=-,\eta,s}^{\dagger}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}_{\mathbf{k}}}}f_{\mathbf{k},2,\eta,s}^{\dagger}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}_{\mathbf{k}}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{G}\\
|\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{G}|<\Lambda_{c}\end{subarray}}\frac{\gamma/v_{\star}}{\eta(k_{x}+G_{x})-i(k_{y}+G_{y})}c_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{G},4,\eta,s}^{\dagger}\ ,

(S124)

where

\mathcal{N}_{\mathbf{k}}=1+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{G}\\
|\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{G}|<\Lambda_{c}\end{subarray}}\frac{\gamma^{2}/v_{\star}^{2}}{|\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{G}|^{2}}\ .

(S125)

The states
d_{\mathbf{k},\zeta,\eta,s}^{\dagger}
are also eigenstate of the (first) chiral operator
C
with eigenvalues equal to
\zeta
.
Their Chern numbers are computed to be
\zeta\cdot\eta
.

In order to calculate the quantum metric tensor, we rewrite the flat band wavefunction in the first-quantized formalism.
For simplicity, here we only focus on the
\zeta=+
,
\eta=+
,
{\varsigma}=\uparrow
sector and will omit the indices
\zeta,\eta,{\varsigma}
.
There is

|u_{\mathbf{k}}\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}_{\mathbf{k}}}}|\widetilde{u}_{\mathbf{k}}\rangle,\qquad|\widetilde{u}_{\mathbf{k}}\rangle=|f_{1},\mathbf{k}\rangle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{G}\\
|\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{G}|<\Lambda_{c}\end{subarray}}\frac{\gamma/v_{\star}}{(k_{x}+G_{x})+i(k_{y}+G_{y})}|c_{3},\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{G}\rangle\ ,

(S126)

where
|f_{1},\mathbf{k}\rangle
and
|c_{3},\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{G}\rangle
are
f
- (
\alpha=1
) and
c
-band basis (
a=3
), respectively, and
|\widetilde{u}_{\mathbf{k}}\rangle
is a holomorphic function of
k_{x}+ik_{y}
.
The quantum metric can be written as a sum of four terms

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}_{ij}(\mathbf{k})=

\displaystyle\langle\partial_{k_{i}}u_{\mathbf{k}}|(1-|u_{\mathbf{k}}\rangle\langle u_{\mathbf{k}}|)|\partial_{k_{j}}u_{\mathbf{k}}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{{\mathcal{N}_{\mathbf{k}}}}\langle\partial_{k_{i}}\widetilde{u}_{\mathbf{k}}|(1-|u_{\mathbf{k}}\rangle\langle u_{\mathbf{k}}|)|\partial_{k_{j}}\widetilde{u}_{\mathbf{k}}\rangle+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}_{\mathbf{k}}}}\left(\partial_{k_{i}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}_{\mathbf{k}}}}\right)\langle\widetilde{u}_{\mathbf{k}}|(1-|u_{\mathbf{k}}\rangle\langle u_{\mathbf{k}}|)|\partial_{k_{j}}\widetilde{u}_{\mathbf{k}}\rangle

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}_{\mathbf{k}}}}\left(\partial_{k_{j}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}_{\mathbf{k}}}}\right)\langle\partial_{k_{i}}\widetilde{u}_{\mathbf{k}}|(1-|u_{\mathbf{k}}\rangle\langle u_{\mathbf{k}}|)|\widetilde{u}_{\mathbf{k}}\rangle+\left(\partial_{k_{i}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}_{\mathbf{k}}}}\right)\left(\partial_{k_{j}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}_{\mathbf{k}}}}\right)\langle\widetilde{u}_{\mathbf{k}}|(1-|u_{\mathbf{k}}\rangle\langle u_{\mathbf{k}}|)|\widetilde{u}_{\mathbf{k}}\rangle\ .

(S127)

Because
(1-|u_{\mathbf{k}}\rangle\langle u_{\mathbf{k}}|)|\widetilde{u}_{\mathbf{k}}\rangle=0
, the last three terms in the above equation vanish.
Hence we have

\mathfrak{g}_{ij}(\mathbf{k})=\frac{1}{{\mathcal{N}_{\mathbf{k}}}}\langle\partial_{k_{i}}\widetilde{u}(\mathbf{k})|(1-|u(\mathbf{k})\rangle\langle u(\mathbf{k})|)|\partial_{k_{j}}\widetilde{u}(\mathbf{k})\rangle\ .

(S128)

Since
\widetilde{u}_{\mathbf{k}}
is holomorphic in
k=k_{x}+ik_{y}
, there are
\partial_{k_{x}}\widetilde{u}_{\mathbf{k}}=\partial_{k}\widetilde{u}_{\mathbf{k}}
,
\partial_{k_{y}}\widetilde{u}_{\mathbf{k}}=i\partial_{k}\widetilde{u}_{\mathbf{k}}
, and the quantum metric must have the form

\mathfrak{g}_{ij}(\mathbf{k})=\begin{pmatrix}1&i\\
-i&1\end{pmatrix}f(\mathbf{k})

(S129)

where
f(\mathbf{k})=\langle\partial_{k}u(\mathbf{k})|(1-|u(\mathbf{k})\rangle\langle u(\mathbf{k})|)|\partial_{k}u(\mathbf{k})\rangle
is a real function of
\mathbf{k}
.
The Fubini-Study metric and Berry’s curvature are given by
g_{ij}(\mathbf{k})=\mathrm{Re}[\mathfrak{g}_{ij}(\mathbf{k})]=f(\mathbf{k})\delta_{ij}
and
\Omega(\mathbf{k})=2\mathrm{Im}[\mathfrak{g}_{xy}(\mathbf{k})]=2f(\mathbf{k})
, respectively, thus the ideal droplet condition
g_{ij}(\mathbf{k})=\frac{1}{2}|\Omega(\mathbf{k})|\delta_{ij}
is satisfied.

We then investigate the flatness of their Berry’s curvatures.
We first consider the parameters
v_{\star}=1.623\mathrm{eV\cdot\mathring{A}}
,
\gamma=-24.75
meV obtained at
w_{0}/w_{1}=0.8
(
table

S4
).
The corresponding Berry’s curvature is shown in
fig.

S5
(b).
We can see that it is not flat at all.
Its deviation from perfect flatness can be estimated by the number

\delta C=\sqrt{\int\frac{d^{2}\mathbf{k}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\left(\frac{\Omega(\mathbf{k})}{2\pi}-C\right)^{2}}\ ,

(S130)

which is computed to be 2.723 for
\gamma=-24.75
meV.
The actual chiral limit of the BM model,
i.e.
,
w_{0}/w_{1}=0
, leads to a larger gap (
100.8
meV) between the flat bands the lowest passive bands.
That means in the actual chiral limit
\gamma
should be
\gamma=-100.8
meV.
For comparison, in
fig.

S5
(c) and (d) we present the distributions of Berry’s curvatures given by
\gamma=-50
meV and
\gamma=-100.8
meV, respectively.
It is clear that a larger
|\gamma|
gives a flatter Berry’s curvature.
The derivations from perfect flatness are 1.1585 and 0.2570 for
\gamma=-50
meV and
\gamma=-100.8
meV, respectively.

In summary, because of the holomorphic or anti-holomorphic property of the flat band wavefunction the ideal droplet condition is automatically achieved in the chiral flat limit of our heavy fermion model, where
v_{\star}^{\prime}
and
M
are artificially imposed to zero.
The relative flatness of the Berry’s curvature can also be reproduced by using parameters fitting the actual BM model bands in the chiral limit.
These lead to the GMP algebra of density operators.
Given that the Hamiltonian (
section

S2.10
) only involves
k_{x}+ik_{y}
or
k_{x}-ik_{y}
and is periodic in real space, the Jastrow type wavefunctions for fractional Chern insulators of spin and valley polarized MATBG
Ledwith et al. 2020
should also be applicable to our model.

Figure S5:
Berry’s curvatures of the flat Chern bands in the (first) chiral flat limit. (a) is an illustration of the moiré Brillouin zone and high symmetry momenta. (b)-(d) are the Berry’s curvatures in the (first) chiral flat limit, where
\gamma
equals to -24.75meV, -50meV, -100.8meV, respectively, and
v_{\star}^{\prime}=M=0
,
v_{\star}=1.623\mathrm{eV\cdot\mathring{A}}
. The values of the Berry’s curvature is normalized in the convention where the area of moiré Brillouin zone is
4\pi^{2}
.

S3
The interaction Hamiltonian and its symmetries

S3.1
The projected density operator and Coulomb interaction

We can project the original basis onto the basis of the topological heavy fermion model as

c_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{Q},\beta,\eta,s}^{\dagger}\approx\sum_{\alpha}\widetilde{v}^{(\eta)*}_{\mathbf{Q}\beta,\alpha}(\mathbf{k})f_{\mathbf{k}\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}+\sum_{a}\widetilde{u}^{(\eta)*}_{\mathbf{Q}\beta,a}(\mathbf{k})c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s}^{\dagger}

(S131)

Correspondingly, the projected real space basis (
eq.

S11
) can be written as

c_{l\beta\eta s}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{r})\approx\sum_{\mathbf{R}\alpha}e^{i\eta\Delta\mathbf{K}_{l}\cdot\mathbf{R}}w^{(\eta)*}_{l\beta,\alpha}(\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{R})f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\Omega_{\rm tot}}}\sum_{|\mathbf{k}|<\Lambda_{c}}\sum_{a}\sum_{\mathbf{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{l\eta}}e^{-i(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{Q})\cdot\mathbf{r}}\widetilde{u}^{(\eta)*}_{\mathbf{Q}\beta,a}(\mathbf{k})c_{\mathbf{k},a\eta s}^{\dagger}\ ,

(S132)

where
\Lambda_{c}
is the cutoff for the conduction band basis.
We emphasize that even though we formally introduce
\widetilde{u}^{(\eta)*}_{\mathbf{Q}\beta,a}(\mathbf{k})
, in practical calculations we will only use
\widetilde{u}^{(\eta)*}_{\mathbf{Q}\beta,a}(0)
, as we did in the k
\cdot
p expansion in
section

S2.3
.
The phase factor
e^{i\eta\Delta\mathbf{K}_{l}\cdot\mathbf{R}}
comes from the phase shift gained by the real space basis under translation, as explained around
eq.

S43
.
We now can write the density operator as

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}(\mathbf{r})=\sum_{\beta l\eta s}c_{l\beta\eta s}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{r})c_{l\beta\eta s}(\mathbf{r})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\beta l\eta s}\bigg[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{R}\alpha\\
\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}\end{subarray}}e^{i\eta\Delta\mathbf{K}_{l}\cdot(\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime})}w^{(\eta)*}_{l\beta,\alpha}(\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{R})w^{(\eta)}_{l\beta,\alpha^{\prime}}(\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime})f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}\eta s}+\frac{1}{\Omega_{\rm tot}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{k}|,|\mathbf{k}^{\prime}|<\Lambda_{c}\\
aa^{\prime}\\
\mathbf{Q}\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{l\eta}\end{subarray}}e^{-i(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{Q}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime}+\mathbf{Q}^{\prime})\cdot\mathbf{r}}\widetilde{u}^{(\eta)*}_{\mathbf{Q}\beta,a}(\mathbf{k})\widetilde{u}^{(\eta)}_{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}\beta,a^{\prime}}(\mathbf{k}^{\prime})c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}a^{\prime}\eta s}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{\Omega_{\rm tot}}}\sum_{\mathbf{R}\alpha a}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{k}|<\Lambda_{c}\\
\mathbf{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{l\eta}\end{subarray}}\left(w^{(\eta)*}_{l\beta,\alpha}(\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{R})\widetilde{u}^{(\eta)}_{\mathbf{Q}\beta,a}(\mathbf{k})e^{i\eta\Delta\mathbf{K}_{l}\cdot\mathbf{R}+i(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{Q})\cdot\mathbf{r}}f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s}+w^{(\eta)}_{l\beta,\alpha}(\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{R})\widetilde{u}^{(\eta)*}_{\mathbf{Q}\beta,a}(\mathbf{k})e^{-i\eta\Delta\mathbf{K}_{l}\cdot\mathbf{R}-i(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{Q})\cdot\mathbf{r}}c^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s}f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}\right)\bigg]

(S133)

Since
w_{l\beta,\alpha}^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{R})
is a sharp Gaussian function centered at
\mathbf{R}
, we can omit the overlap between Wannier functions at different sites,
i.e.
,
w^{(\eta)*}_{l\beta,\alpha}(\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{R})w^{(\eta)}_{l\beta,\alpha^{\prime}}(\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime})
for
\mathbf{R}\neq\mathbf{R}^{\prime}
.
From
eqs.

S53
and
S54
we find
\sum_{l\beta}w^{(\eta)*}_{l\beta,\alpha}(\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{R})w^{(\eta)}_{l\beta,\alpha^{\prime}}(\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{R})
is zero if
\alpha\neq\alpha^{\prime}
.
This property is guaranteed by the
PC_{2z}T
symmetry, which implies the constraint
eq.

S49
and hence

\displaystyle\sum_{l\beta}w^{(\eta)*}_{l\beta,\alpha}(\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{R})w^{(\eta)}_{l\beta,\overline{\alpha}}(\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{R})=\sum_{l\beta}il\eta(-1)^{\alpha}w^{(\eta)*}_{-l\overline{\beta},\overline{\alpha}}(\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{R})(-i)l\eta(-1)^{\overline{\alpha}}w^{(\eta)}_{-l\overline{\beta},{\alpha}}(\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{R})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sum_{l\beta}w^{(\eta)*}_{-l\overline{\beta},\overline{\alpha}}(\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{R})w^{(\eta)}_{-l\overline{\beta},{\alpha}}(\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{R})=-\sum_{l\beta}w^{(\eta)*}_{l\beta,\alpha}(\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{R})w^{(\eta)}_{l\beta,\overline{\alpha}}(\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{R})\ =0\ .

(S134)

We can understand this property as a consequence of the (single-particle) anti-unitary particle-hole symmetry
{\mathcal{P}}=PC_{2z}T
.
According to the representations in
eq.

S36
,
D^{f}(P)=i\sigma_{z}\tau_{z}
,
D^{f}(C_{2z}T)=\sigma_{x}\tau_{0}
, there is
D^{f}({\mathcal{P}})=-\sigma_{y}\tau_{z}
.
Since
{\mathcal{P}}^{2}=-1
and
|W_{\mathbf{R}2\eta s}\rangle=-i\eta{\mathcal{P}}|W_{\mathbf{R}1\eta s}\rangle
there must be
\langle W_{\mathbf{R}2\eta s}|O|W_{\mathbf{R}1\eta s}\rangle=0
for any hermitian operator that commutes with
{\mathcal{P}}
(Kramers theorem).

section

S3.1
can be obtained if we choose
O
as the density operator at
\mathbf{r}
.
According to the constraints
eqs.

S46
and
S49
, the density of the Wannier functions

n_{f}(\mathbf{r})=\sum_{l\beta}|w_{l\beta,\alpha}^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{r})|^{2}

(S135)

does not depend on
\alpha
and
\eta
.
Therefore, we can simplify the density operator as

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}(\mathbf{r})=\sum_{\eta s}\bigg[\sum_{\mathbf{R}\alpha}n_{f}(\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{R})f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}+\frac{1}{\Omega_{\rm tot}}\sum_{l\beta aa^{\prime}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{k}|,|\mathbf{k}^{\prime}|<\Lambda_{c}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\mathbf{Q}\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{l\eta}}e^{-i(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{Q}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime}+\mathbf{Q}^{\prime})\cdot\mathbf{r}}\widetilde{u}^{(\eta)*}_{\mathbf{Q}\beta,a}(\mathbf{k})\widetilde{u}^{(\eta)}_{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}\beta,a^{\prime}}(\mathbf{k}^{\prime})c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}a^{\prime}\eta s}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\Omega_{\rm tot}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l\beta\\
\mathbf{R}\alpha a\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{k}|<\Lambda_{c}\\
\mathbf{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{l\eta}\end{subarray}}\left(w^{(\eta)*}_{l\beta,\alpha}(\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{R})\widetilde{u}^{(\eta)}_{\mathbf{Q}\beta,a}(\mathbf{k})e^{i\eta\Delta\mathbf{K}_{l}\cdot\mathbf{R}+i(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{Q})\cdot\mathbf{r}}f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s}+w^{(\eta)}_{l\beta,\alpha}(\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{R})\widetilde{u}^{(\eta)*}_{\mathbf{Q}\beta,a}(\mathbf{k})e^{-i\eta\Delta\mathbf{K}_{l}\cdot\mathbf{R}-i(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{Q})\cdot\mathbf{r}}c^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s}f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}\right)\bigg]\ .

(S136)

We now consider the interaction Hamiltonian of the form

\hat{H}_{I}=\frac{1}{2}\int d^{2}\mathbf{r}_{1}d^{2}\mathbf{r}_{2}V(\mathbf{r}_{1}-\mathbf{r}_{2}):\hat{\rho}(\mathbf{r}_{1})::\hat{\rho}(\mathbf{r}_{2}):

(S137)

where
V
is the double-gate-screened Coulomb interaction (will be defined in next paragraph) and
:\hat{\rho}:=\hat{\rho}-\langle G_{0}|\hat{\rho}|G_{0}\rangle
is the normal ordered density operator with respect to a state
|G_{0}\rangle
at charge neutrality point.
We assume
|G_{0}\rangle
is such a state that

\langle G_{0}|c_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{Q},\alpha,\eta,s}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},\mathbf{Q}^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime},\eta^{\prime},s^{\prime}}|G_{0}\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\delta_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\delta_{\mathbf{Q}\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}}\delta_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\delta_{\eta\eta^{\prime}}\delta_{ss^{\prime}}\ .

(S138)

One can verify

\langle G_{0}|f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}|G_{0}\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\delta_{\mathbf{R}\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}\delta_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\delta_{\eta\eta^{\prime}}\delta_{ss^{\prime}},\qquad\langle G_{0}|c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}a^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}|G_{0}\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\delta_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\delta_{aa^{\prime}}\delta_{\eta\eta^{\prime}}\delta_{ss^{\prime}}\ ,

(S139)

\langle G_{0}|f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}a^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}|G_{0}\rangle=\langle G_{0}|c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}|G_{0}\rangle=0\ .

(S140)

Therefore, we can write the normal ordered density operator as

\displaystyle:\hat{\rho}(\mathbf{r}):=\sum_{\eta s}\bigg[\sum_{\mathbf{R}\alpha}n_{f}(\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{R}):f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}:+\frac{1}{\Omega_{\rm tot}}\sum_{l\beta aa^{\prime}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{k}|,|\mathbf{k}^{\prime}|<\Lambda_{c}\\
\mathbf{Q}\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{l\eta}\end{subarray}}e^{-i(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{Q}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime}+\mathbf{Q}^{\prime})\cdot\mathbf{r}}\widetilde{u}^{(\eta)*}_{\mathbf{Q}\beta,a}(\mathbf{k})\widetilde{u}^{(\eta)}_{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}\beta,a^{\prime}}(\mathbf{k}^{\prime}):c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}a^{\prime}\eta s}:

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\Omega_{\rm tot}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l\beta\\
\mathbf{R}\alpha a\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{k}|<\Lambda_{c}\\
\mathbf{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{l\eta}\end{subarray}}\left(w^{(\eta)*}_{l\beta,\alpha}(\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{R})\widetilde{u}^{(\eta)}_{\mathbf{Q}\beta,a}(\mathbf{k})e^{i\eta\Delta\mathbf{K}_{l}\cdot\mathbf{R}+i(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{Q})\cdot\mathbf{r}}f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s}+w^{(\eta)}_{l\beta,\alpha}(\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{R})\widetilde{u}^{(\eta)*}_{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}\beta,a^{\prime}}(\mathbf{k})e^{-i\eta\Delta\mathbf{K}_{l}\cdot\mathbf{R}-i(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{Q})\cdot\mathbf{r}}c^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s}f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}\right)\bigg]\ .

(S141)

where

:f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}:=f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}-\frac{1}{2},\qquad:c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}a^{\prime}\eta s}:=c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}a^{\prime}\eta s}-\frac{1}{2}\delta_{aa^{\prime}}\ ,

(S142)

:c^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}\eta s}f_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}:=c^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}\eta s}f_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}},\qquad:f_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}c^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}\eta s}:=f_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}c^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}\eta s}\ .

(S143)

The double-gate-screened Coulomb interaction is given by

V(\mathbf{r})=U_{\xi}\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{n}}{\sqrt{(\mathbf{r}/\xi)^{2}+n^{2}}}

(S144)

with
\xi
being distance between the two gates,
U_{\xi}=e^{2}/(4\pi\epsilon\xi)
, and
\epsilon\approx 6
is the dielectric constant.
For
\xi=10
nm there is
U_{\xi}=24
meV.
For convenience we introduce the Fourier transformation of
V(\mathbf{r})
as

V(\mathbf{r})=\int\frac{d^{2}\mathbf{q}}{(2\pi)^{2}}V(\mathbf{q})e^{-i\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{r}},\qquad V(\mathbf{q})={\pi\xi^{2}U_{\xi}}\cdot\frac{\tanh(\xi|\mathbf{q}|/2)}{\xi|\mathbf{q}|/2}\ .

(S145)

For simplicity, we denote the four terms in
section

S3.1
as
:\rho_{ff}:
,
:\rho_{cc}:
,
:\rho_{fc}:
,
:\rho_{cf}:
, respectively.
Their explicit forms are given by

:\rho_{ff}:=\sum_{\eta s}\sum_{\mathbf{R}\alpha}n_{f}(\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{R}):f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}:\ ,

(S146)

:\rho_{cc}:=\frac{1}{\Omega_{\rm tot}}\sum_{\eta s}\sum_{l\beta aa^{\prime}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{k}|,|\mathbf{k}^{\prime}|<\Lambda_{c}\\
\mathbf{Q}\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{l\eta}\end{subarray}}e^{-i(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{Q}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime}+\mathbf{Q}^{\prime})\cdot\mathbf{r}}\widetilde{u}^{(\eta)*}_{\mathbf{Q}\beta,a}(\mathbf{k})\widetilde{u}^{(\eta)}_{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}\beta,a^{\prime}}(\mathbf{k}^{\prime}):c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}a^{\prime}\eta s}:\ ,

(S147)

:\rho_{fc}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\Omega_{\rm tot}}}\sum_{\eta s}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l\beta\\
\mathbf{R}\alpha a\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{k}|<\Lambda_{c}\\
\mathbf{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{l\eta}\end{subarray}}w^{(\eta)*}_{l\beta,\alpha}(\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{R})\widetilde{u}^{(\eta)}_{\mathbf{Q}\beta,a}(\mathbf{k})e^{i\eta\Delta\mathbf{K}_{l}\cdot\mathbf{R}+i(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{Q})\cdot\mathbf{r}}f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s},\qquad:\rho_{cf}:=(:\rho_{fc}:)^{\dagger}\ .

(S148)

There are four diagonal terms in the interaction:

1.

The term
:\rho_{ff}(\mathbf{r}_{1}):V(\mathbf{r}_{1}-\mathbf{r}_{2}):\rho_{ff}(\mathbf{r}_{2}):
is a density-density interaction of the local
f
-orbitals. As will be shown in
section

S3.2
, the on-site repulsion (
U_{1}
) is the largest energy scale of the problem.

2.

The term
:\rho_{cc}(\mathbf{r}_{1}):V(\mathbf{r}_{1}-\mathbf{r}_{2}):\rho_{cc}(\mathbf{r}_{2}):
is the density-density interaction of the conduction bands.

3.

The term
:\rho_{fc}(\mathbf{r}_{1}):V(\mathbf{r}_{1}-\mathbf{r}_{2}):\rho_{fc}(\mathbf{r}_{2}):
creates two particles in the local orbitals and two holes in the conduction bands. Exciting particles or holes in the local orbitals must overcome a large repulsion energy of the
f
-electrons. Thus this term is a high energy process. We will omit this term in the Hartree-Fock calculation.

4.

The term
:\rho_{cf}(\mathbf{r}_{1}):V(\mathbf{r}_{1}-\mathbf{r}_{2}):\rho_{cf}(\mathbf{r}_{2}):
creates two particles in the conduction bands and two holes in the local orbitals.

There are also
\begin{pmatrix}4\\
2\end{pmatrix}=6
off-diagonal terms in the interaction:

5.

The term
:\rho_{ff}(\mathbf{r}_{1}):V(\mathbf{r}_{1}-\mathbf{r}_{2}):\rho_{cc}(\mathbf{r}_{2}):+(\leftrightarrow)
is the density-density interaction between the local orbitals and the conduction bands. This term will effectively shift the energy of conduction bands if
:\rho_{ff}:\neq 0
.

6.

The term
:\rho_{ff}(\mathbf{r}_{1}):V(\mathbf{r}_{1}-\mathbf{r}_{2}):\rho_{fc}(\mathbf{r}_{2}):+(\leftrightarrow)
creates two particles plus one hole in the local orbitals and a hole in the conduction bands.
As shown in
section

S3.5
, this term is guaranteed to be vanishing by the symmetries.

7.

The term
:\rho_{ff}(\mathbf{r}_{1}):V(\mathbf{r}_{1}-\mathbf{r}_{2}):\rho_{cf}(\mathbf{r}_{2}):+(\leftrightarrow)
creates two holes plus a particle in the local orbitals and a particle in the conduction bands.
As the 6th term, this term is guaranteed to be vanishing by the symmetries.

8.

The term
:\rho_{cc}(\mathbf{r}_{1}):V(\mathbf{r}_{1}-\mathbf{r}_{2}):\rho_{fc}(\mathbf{r}_{2}):+(\leftrightarrow)
creates a particle in the local orbitals and two holes plus one particle in the conduction bands. Exciting particles or holes in the local orbitals must overcome a large repulsion energy of the
f
-electrons. Thus this term is a high energy process. This term is also weak because the hybridization,
i.e.
,
\langle f^{\dagger}c\rangle
, is small, as discussed in
section

S4
. We will omit this term in the Hartree-Fock calculation.

9.

The term
:\rho_{cc}(\mathbf{r}_{1}):V(\mathbf{r}_{1}-\mathbf{r}_{2}):\rho_{cf}(\mathbf{r}_{2}):+(\leftrightarrow)
creates a hole in the local orbitals and two particles plus one hole in the conduction bands. Exciting particles or holes in the local orbitals must overcome a large repulsion energy of the
f
-electrons. Thus this term is a high energy process. This term is also weak because the hybridization,
i.e.
,
\langle c^{\dagger}f\rangle
, is small, as discussed in
section

S4
. We will omit this term in the Hartree-Fock calculation.

10.

The term
:\rho_{fc}(\mathbf{r}_{1}):V(\mathbf{r}_{1}-\mathbf{r}_{2}):\rho_{cf}(\mathbf{r}_{2}):+(\leftrightarrow)
is an exchange interaction between local orbitals and conduction bands.

We will derive their explicit forms and calculate their interaction strengths in
sections

S3.2
,
S3.5
,
S3.3
and
S3.4
.

S3.2
Density-density terms (the first and fifth terms)

S3.2.1
The density-density terms of local orbitals (the first term)

We can write the density-density interaction of the local orbitals,
i.e.
, the first term discussed in
section

S3.1
, as

\displaystyle{\hat{H}}_{U}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int d^{2}\mathbf{r}_{1}d^{2}\mathbf{r}_{2}V(\mathbf{r}_{1}-\mathbf{r}_{2}):\hat{\rho}_{ff}(\mathbf{r}_{1})::\hat{\rho}_{ff}(\mathbf{r}_{2}):=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\mathbf{R}\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}\sum_{\alpha\eta s}\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}U(\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime}):f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}::f_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}:\ ,

(S149)

where

U(\mathbf{R})=\int d^{2}\mathbf{r}_{1}d^{2}\mathbf{r}_{2}\ V(\mathbf{r}_{1}-\mathbf{r}_{2}-\mathbf{R})n_{f}(\mathbf{r}_{1})n_{f}(\mathbf{r}_{2})\ .

(S150)

We will not apply the above equation for practical calculation of
U(\mathbf{R})
because
V(\mathbf{r})
is divergent when
\mathbf{r}
approaches zero.
Instead we calculate
U(\mathbf{R})
in momentum space.
According to the transformation (
eq.

S44
)

w_{l\beta,\alpha}^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{r})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N\Omega_{\rm tot}}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}\sum_{\mathbf{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{l\eta}}e^{i(\eta\Delta\mathbf{K}_{l}+\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{Q})\cdot\mathbf{r}}\widetilde{v}^{(\eta)}_{\mathbf{Q}\beta,\alpha}(\mathbf{k})\ ,

(S151)

where
\Omega_{\rm tot}=N\Omega_{0}
,
we can express the density function (
eq.

S135
) as

n_{f}(\mathbf{r})=\frac{1}{N^{2}\Omega_{0}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\sum_{l\beta}\sum_{\mathbf{Q},\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{l\eta}}e^{i(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{Q}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime}+\mathbf{Q}^{\prime})\cdot\mathbf{r}}\widetilde{v}^{(\eta)*}_{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}\beta,\alpha}(\mathbf{k}^{\prime})\widetilde{v}^{(\eta)}_{\mathbf{Q}\beta,\alpha}(\mathbf{k})\ .

(S152)

We define the Fourier transformation of the density function as

n_{f}(\mathbf{r})=\frac{1}{N\Omega_{0}}\sum_{\mathbf{q},\mathbf{G}}n_{f}(\mathbf{q}+\mathbf{G})e^{-i(\mathbf{q}+\mathbf{G})\cdot\mathbf{r}},\qquad n_{f}(\mathbf{q}+\mathbf{G})=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}\sum_{\mathbf{Q}\beta}\widetilde{v}^{(\eta)*}_{\mathbf{Q}-\mathbf{G}\beta,\alpha}(\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{q})\widetilde{v}^{(\eta)}_{\mathbf{Q}\beta,\alpha}(\mathbf{k})\ .

(S153)

The function
U(\mathbf{R})
can be calculated as

\displaystyle U(\mathbf{R})=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N^{2}\Omega_{0}^{2}}\int d^{2}\mathbf{r}_{1}d^{2}\mathbf{r}_{2}\ V(\mathbf{r}_{1}-\mathbf{r}_{2}-\mathbf{R})\sum_{\mathbf{q}\mathbf{G}}\sum_{\mathbf{q}^{\prime}\mathbf{G}^{\prime}}e^{-i(\mathbf{q}+\mathbf{G})\cdot\mathbf{r}_{1}-i(\mathbf{q}^{\prime}+\mathbf{G}^{\prime})\cdot\mathbf{r}_{2}}n_{f}(\mathbf{q}+\mathbf{G})n_{f}(\mathbf{q}^{\prime}+\mathbf{G}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N^{2}\Omega_{0}^{2}}\int d^{2}\mathbf{r}_{2}\ \sum_{\mathbf{q}\mathbf{G}}\sum_{\mathbf{q}^{\prime}\mathbf{G}^{\prime}}V(\mathbf{q}+\mathbf{G})e^{-i(\mathbf{q}+\mathbf{G}+\mathbf{q}^{\prime}+\mathbf{G}^{\prime})\cdot\mathbf{r}_{2}-i(\mathbf{q}+\mathbf{G})\cdot\mathbf{R}}n_{f}(\mathbf{q}+\mathbf{G})n_{f}(\mathbf{q}^{\prime}+\mathbf{G}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N\Omega_{0}}\sum_{\mathbf{q}\mathbf{G}}V(\mathbf{q}+\mathbf{G})e^{-i\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{R}}n_{f}(\mathbf{q}+\mathbf{G})n_{f}(-\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{G})\ .

(S154)

For
w_{0}/w_{1}=0.8
, we find that the onsite, nearest neighbor, next-nearest neighbor, and third nearest neighbor interactions are

U(0)=57.95\mathrm{meV},\qquad U(\mathbf{a}_{M1})=2.157\mathrm{meV},\qquad U(\mathbf{a}_{M1}-\mathbf{a}_{M2})=0.1144\mathrm{meV},\qquad U(2\mathbf{a}_{M1})=0.04145\mathrm{meV}\ ,

(S155)

respectively.
We can see that the interaction decays quickly as
\mathbf{R}
and the onsite term (
U_{1}=U(0)
) is much larger than the other terms.
In fact, we find that
U_{1}
is the largest energy scale in this problem.
Thus the onsite interaction will dominate the correlation physics.
Nevertheless, the
U(\mathbf{R})
(
\mathbf{R}\neq 0
) terms will contribute to a chemical potential term (for the local orbitals) in the
U_{1}\to\infty
limit.
Suppose
U_{1}
has frozen the charge fluctuation of local orbitals,
i.e.
,
\forall\mathbf{R}
,
\sum_{\alpha\eta s}\langle f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}\rangle=N_{f}
is constant, then all terms in
eq.

S149
with
\mathbf{R}\neq\mathbf{R}^{\prime}
can be decoupled in the Hartree channel as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\mathbf{R}\neq\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}\sum_{\alpha\eta s}\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}U(\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime}):f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}::f_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}:

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\mathbf{R}\neq\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}\sum_{\alpha\eta s}\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}U(\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime})\left(2\langle:f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}:\rangle:f_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}:-\langle:f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}:\rangle\langle:f_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}:\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left((N_{f}-4)\sum_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\neq 0}U(\mathbf{R}^{\prime})\right)\sum_{\mathbf{R}}\sum_{\alpha\eta s}f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}+const.

(S156)

In order to capture the physics of chemical potential shift in an as simple as possible model, we consider the approximate Hamiltonian of the form

{\hat{H}}_{U}=\frac{U_{1}}{2}\sum_{\mathbf{R}}\sum_{\alpha\eta s}\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}:f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}::f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}:+\frac{U_{2}}{2}\sum_{\langle\mathbf{R}\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\rangle}\sum_{\alpha\eta s}\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}:f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}::f_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}:\ ,

(S157)

where
\langle\mathbf{R}\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\rangle
only sums over nearest neighbor pairs on the triangular lattice.
Rather than naively choosing
U_{2}=U(\mathbf{a}_{M1})
, we choose
U_{2}
as such a value that it gives the same chemical potential shift as
section

S3.2.1
.
In other words, we absorb all the chemical potential shift effects coming from the
U(\mathbf{R})
(
\mathbf{R}\neq 0
) terms into the nearest neighbor repulsion.

U_{2}
can be calculated as

U_{2}=\frac{1}{6}\sum_{\mathbf{R}\neq 0}U(\mathbf{R})=\frac{1}{6}\left(-U_{1}+\frac{1}{\Omega_{0}}\sum_{\mathbf{G}}V(\mathbf{G})n_{f}(\mathbf{G})n_{f}(-\mathbf{G})\right)\ .

(S158)

For
w_{0}/w_{1}=0.8
we find
U_{2}=2.329
meV, which is only slightly larger than
U(\mathbf{a}_{M1})
.

Table S6:
Parameters of the interaction Hamiltonian.
Parameters of the BM model used to obtain this table are
v_{F}=5.944\mathrm{eV\cdot\mathring{A}}
,
|\Delta\mathbf{K}|=1.703\mathrm{\mathring{A}^{-1}}
,
w_{1}=110\mathrm{meV}
,
\theta=1.05^{\circ}
.
Parameters of the double-gate-screened interaction used to obtain this table are
U_{\xi}=24{\rm meV}
,
\xi=10{\rm nm}
.

S3.2.2
The density-density terms between local orbitals and conduction bands (the fifth term)

Second we consider the interaction between local orbital density and conduction electron density

\displaystyle{\hat{H}}_{W}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\Omega_{\rm tot}}\int d^{2}\mathbf{r}_{1}d^{2}\mathbf{r}_{2}\sum_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta_{1}s_{1}}:f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta_{1}s_{1}}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta_{1}s_{1}}:n_{f}(\mathbf{r}_{1}-\mathbf{R})V(\mathbf{r}_{1}-\mathbf{r}_{2})\sum_{|\mathbf{k}|,|\mathbf{k}^{\prime}|<\Lambda_{c}}\sum_{\eta_{2}s_{2}aa^{\prime}}\sum_{l\beta}\sum_{\mathbf{Q},\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{l\eta_{2}}}

\displaystyle\times\widetilde{u}^{(\eta_{2})*}_{\mathbf{Q}\beta,a}(\mathbf{k})\widetilde{u}^{(\eta_{2})}_{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}\beta,a^{\prime}}(\mathbf{k}^{\prime})e^{-i(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{Q}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime}+\mathbf{Q}^{\prime})\cdot\mathbf{r}_{2}}:c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta_{2}s_{2}}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}a^{\prime}\eta_{2}s_{2}}:

(S159)

Applying the transformation (
eq.

S145
)
